## [RoleWorld Uses Functional Role Abstractions for Knowledge Transfer in LLM World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13172) ⭐️ 7.0/10

RoleWorld introduces functional role abstractions—reusable prototypes paired with environment-specific parameters—to represent how objects behave in interactions, enabling symbolic world models for LLM agents to transfer knowledge across environments. A teacher LLM extracts role prototypes and executable transition rules from interaction trajectories, and in a target world the actor LLM uses this skill with online role assignments and parameter estimates, without updating LLM parameters or transition rules. This addresses a real limitation in LLM agent generalization: prior symbolic world models organize knowledge around environment-specific objects, which hinders transfer to new environments with similar interaction structure but altered object behavior. RoleWorld's functional role abstractions could make LLM agents more robust and sample-efficient in unfamiliar settings, with potential impact on planning, robotics, and multi-agent research. The approach keeps transition rules fixed and performs no LLM parameter updates; instead, feedback from each action refines role assignments and parameter values online, and the actor actively tests a role instance hypothesis through interaction when its uncertainty affects the current decision. Experiments on the MARS benchmark show robust generalization across environments with significantly altered object behavior.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: World models allow LLM agents to anticipate the effects of their actions and make informed decisions. Symbolic world models encode dynamics as rules or executable programs, whose compositional structure in principle supports transfer, but they typically organize knowledge around environment-specific objects. RoleWorld builds on this line of work by introducing reusable role prototypes and environment-specific parameters as functional role abstractions.

## [AdaC-GRPO Uses Adaptive Curriculum to Solve Visual Sudoku Puzzles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13184) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces AdaC-GRPO, an adaptive curriculum learning method that dynamically builds training batches based on the model's Zone of Proximal Development, along with FG-Sudoku, a fine-grained benchmark categorized by grid size and required deductive steps. Experiments show that AdaC-GRPO enables Qwen2.5-VL-7B to approach Gemini-2.5-Flash performance and bridge 62.2% of the accuracy gap to Gemini-2.5-Pro, with generalizability validated on InternVL-3.5 and Qwen3-VL. This work addresses reward sparsity in long-horizon multimodal reinforcement learning, a key bottleneck for training MLLMs on complex reasoning tasks. The adaptive curriculum approach could generalize to other domains where standard RLVR fails due to near-zero success rates, potentially advancing multimodal reasoning capabilities. FG-Sudoku is categorized by grid size and required deductive steps, revealing that frontier MLLMs fail on complex long-range dependencies such as 9x9 puzzles. AdaC-GRPO dynamically constructs training batches by tracking online rollout accuracy to identify the model's Zone of Proximal Development, unlike rigid or prior-based schedules.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Reinforcement learning with verifiable reward (RLVR) is a technique that uses automatically checkable ground-truth criteria to train large language models, enhancing their reasoning abilities. Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) is an efficient RL algorithm originally developed for improving reasoning in LLMs. The Zone of Proximal Development (ZPD) is a pedagogical concept describing the gap between what a learner can do alone and what they can achieve with support; adaptive systems use ZPD to select optimally challenging tasks. Visual Sudoku puzzles require multi-step deductive reasoning over both local constraints and global dependency chains, making them a representative challenge for long-horizon multimodal tasks.

## [JANUS Uses Co-Evolving World Models for Long-Horizon Autonomous Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13194) ⭐️ 7.0/10

JANUS introduces a Predict-Verify-Evolve loop driven by a co-evolving world model that forecasts value improvement, information gain, and exploration utility to select, refine, or prune research branches before costly execution. Evaluations on MLS-Bench-Lite and real research tasks show it significantly outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in both evolving efficiency and ceiling. This addresses a core bottleneck in autonomous AI research: the Generate-Execute-Feedback paradigm relies on slow, costly trial-and-error that throttles progress as the search space explodes. By enabling agents to forecast and reflect before executing, JANUS could accelerate AI-driven scientific discovery and reduce compute costs for long-horizon research agents. The framework distills experience into hierarchical skills for the research agent, exploration-strategy skills for the world model, and coordination protocols between them, enabling continual co-evolution. As a preprint without peer review, its claims still require independent validation and community discussion.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Long-horizon autonomous research refers to AI agents that sustain coherent progress across many sequential steps—such as hypothesis generation, implementation, experimentation, and debugging—over hours or days. World models are learned predictive models of an environment that let agents simulate outcomes before acting, and co-evolving means the agent and world model improve each other iteratively. The Generate-Execute-Feedback paradigm is the standard loop where an agent proposes an action, executes it, and learns from the result, but execution delays make this inefficient at scale.

## [DREAM: Neuro-Inspired Memory Repair for Poisoned LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13267) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced DREAM (Dynamic Reactivation for Engram Attenuation in Memory), a training-free, agent-intrinsic memory repair framework that selectively forgets poisoned memories in LLM agents. It uses a three-stage pipeline — perturbation-induced implicit reactivation, adaptive anomaly diagnosis, and dynamic memory repair — reducing backdoor poisoning attack success rates by over 95% while preserving benign utility. Memory poisoning is a critical security threat to LLM agents with persistent memory, since a single adversarial memory write can hijack future behavior across sessions. DREAM offers a more efficient defense than existing external-module approaches, achieving up to 2.13× speedup and 37.7% token reduction versus A-MemGuard, which matters for deploying safe agents in real-world systems. DREAM is training-free and agent-intrinsic, avoiding the low memory efficiency and high computational overhead of static isolation or continuous online auditing. It also achieves competitive robustness against injection attacks and a 92.96% task success rate on poisoned multi-agent systems.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: LLM agents increasingly use persistent memory to retain context across interactions, but this memory can be poisoned by adversaries who inject malicious instructions through query-only interactions, corrupting long-term behavior. Existing defenses typically rely on external modules for static memory isolation or continuous online auditing, which are inefficient. DREAM draws on neuroscience findings that weak reactivation can destabilize memories and induce decay without reinforcement, applying this idea to selectively forget harmful memories.

## [BoundaryEvo Co-Evolves Data and Model for Long-Chain Reasoning Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13285) ⭐️ 7.0/10

BoundaryEvo introduces a two-stage data-model co-evolution framework for distilling long-chain reasoning into small language models, first priming basic reasoning capability and then repeatedly selecting questions at the student's evolving capability boundary. Using only 1,000 training examples, it raises Qwen3.5-4B accuracy from 62.9 to 75.3 on IMO-AnswerBench and from 26.8 to 46.0 on AMO-Bench across 11 reasoning benchmarks. Most existing distillation pipelines fix the training set before training begins, which wastes limited teacher trajectories because an example's usefulness changes as the student improves. By adapting data selection to the student's evolving capability boundary, BoundaryEvo offers a more sample-efficient recipe that could influence future work on efficient reasoning distillation for small models. The method avoids a separate judge model during its Capability Priming stage, directly distilling a small set of teacher-generated long reasoning trajectories, and then in the co-evolution stage repeatedly selects questions the current student has not mastered but the expert can reliably solve. The results are reported on an arXiv preprint without peer review, and the code and data are promised to be released publicly.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Long-chain reasoning distillation transfers the multi-step reasoning traces of large teacher models to smaller students, a technique that has proven effective for improving small-model reasoning. A recurring challenge is that a fixed training set becomes mismatched to the student over time: already-mastered examples provide little new supervision, while examples far beyond the student's ability are hard to learn from. BoundaryEvo addresses this by co-evolving the data with the student's capability boundary, a concept related to prior work on data-model co-evolution and capability-boundary estimation in language models.

## [ChemDiffAgent: First Masked Diffusion Language Agent for Chemical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13330) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced ChemDiffAgent, the first masked diffusion language agent for tool-integrated chemical reasoning, which reformulates reasoning as iterative denoising over interleaved reasoning and tool-use trajectories. It uses a two-stage post-training framework (agentic supervised fine-tuning plus variance-reduced preference optimization) and outperforms autoregressive agents by roughly 20% overall, with an 8B model matching or surpassing larger frontier models like GPT 5.5 and Claude Opus 4.7. This work challenges the dominant autoregressive paradigm in agent design by showing that masked diffusion can enable joint, full-context tool-use decisions, which could improve AI-for-science agents that must integrate multi-source evidence. If the results generalize, it may shift how tool-integrated reasoning agents are built across chemistry and other scientific domains. The approach benefits from harder infilling subproblems during training and flexible arbitrary-order decoding at inference, allowing tool-use decisions to be jointly determined under full-trajectory context. The authors also built a benchmark covering six chemistry tasks in both single-turn and multi-turn tool-use scenarios, and released code at https://github.com/bilemond/ChemDiffAgent.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Autoregressive language models generate text left-to-right using causal attention, meaning each token can only attend to previous tokens; this limits bidirectional understanding and global planning. Masked diffusion language models instead generate text by iteratively denoising masked tokens, allowing them to fill in or revise any position in parallel. ChemDiffAgent applies this diffusion paradigm to chemical agents that call external tools, aiming to better align the agent's decision boundary with its knowledge boundary.

## [SWE-Critix Trains Generalist Verifiers for Coding-Agent Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13356) ⭐️ 7.0/10

SWE-Critix introduces a post-training pipeline that combines supervised fine-tuning (SFT) with reinforcement learning (RL) to train generalist verifiers that judge and explain coding-agent execution trajectories. On a held-out test set, the 30B model improves classification accuracy from 57% for the base Qwen3-30B-A3B reasoning model to 66% after SFT and 78% after RL, and the authors release their models, datasets, and training code. As coding agents take on long-horizon, end-to-end software engineering tasks, there is often no ready oracle evaluator to define success, so reliable verifiers become essential for evaluation and for reinforcement-learning training signals. This work shows that training-based generalist verification can substantially outperform an untuned general-purpose LLM, which could improve how agent trajectories are scored and how agents themselves are trained. The verifier takes a task and its execution trajectory as input, predicts a completion label, and generates a natural-language analysis explaining its judgment; the pipeline trains a series of 30B models on trajectories from CodeForge-Preview. The trajectories can be extremely long, containing hundreds of iterative interactions with code repositories and external tools, which is the core challenge the paper addresses.

**Background**: Coding agents are LLM-based systems that autonomously edit repositories, run tests, and call external tools to complete software engineering tasks. Post-training refers to adapting a pre-trained LLM through methods such as supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning (RL) to specialize its behavior. A verifier is a model that judges whether an agent's work is correct; unlike a scalar reward model, a generalist verifier also explains its reasoning, which matters when no ground-truth evaluator exists.

## [Web$hell Unifies Search, Browsing, and Evidence for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13416) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2610.13416) introduces Web$hell, an interface that unifies web access for LLM agents under a single access model. It uses WebContract as a common schema, WebCompiler to compile live webpages into addressable information and action objects, and WebCache to persist them, with experiments showing strong performance at lower cost across web search, navigation, and deep research benchmarks. Existing agentic web access models—search-augmented, browser-based, and filesystem-based agents—each support only part of long-horizon tasks, forcing costly trade-offs when agents must gather information, act, and revisit evidence in one trajectory. A unified interface could make LLM agents more capable and efficient on end-to-end web tasks, influencing how future agent frameworks are designed. Web$hell's key mechanism is compiling live webpages into addressable information and action objects maintained in WebCache and manipulated through a shared set of operations, giving agents consistent, operable, and persistent access. The paper reports extensive experiments across web search, web navigation, and deep research benchmarks, though as a preprint it has not yet undergone peer review.

## [Local Diagnostics of Learned Event Representations for HL-LHC Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13437) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper evaluates supervised and self-supervised neural embedding strategies for anomaly detection on COLLIDE-2V, a 750-million-event simulated dataset under realistic High-Luminosity LHC conditions, using autoencoder reconstruction error in the learned latent space as the anomaly score. It introduces a local diagnostic pipeline that fits a Gaussian Mixture Model to the Standard Model latent space, profiles each component through high-level observables without process labels, localizes flagged events, and ranks observables by local discriminating power. Anomaly detection is a promising model-agnostic strategy for new-physics searches at the LHC, but learned low-dimensional representations sacrifice the physical interpretability of hand-crafted observables. This work addresses that gap by enabling local diagnosis of flagged events, which is necessary to distinguish potential new physics from mismodeling or detector effects and to identify observables for targeted follow-up searches. Supervised objectives that exploit simulation process labels generally outperform self-supervised counterparts and, in their best configurations, improve on an autoencoder trained directly on kinematic features, raising signal efficiency at 10% background acceptance on all held-out processes. The diagnostic ranking recovers the defining kinematics of held-out processes, and correlating the anomaly score with the same observables reveals cases where the most discriminating observable is not the one the score follows most closely.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: The High-Luminosity LHC (HL-LHC) is a major upgrade to CERN's Large Hadron Collider that aims to increase instantaneous luminosity by a factor of 5 to 7.5, with physics experiments expected to start taking data around 2030. Anomaly detection with autoencoders works by training a network to reconstruct normal (Standard Model) events and flagging events with high reconstruction error as anomalous. COLLIDE-2V is a large simulated dataset of 750 million collision events generated under realistic HL-LHC conditions for developing and benchmarking such machine-learning methods.

## [Rollout-Time Intervention Boosts Agentic RL Beyond Policy Limits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13438) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2610.13438) introduces Rollout-Time Intervention (RTI), a technique that uses reward-side evidence such as evaluation rubrics during rollout to convert erroneous trajectories into successful ones, generating learning signals for tasks the policy rarely solves on its own. Experiments across five benchmarks show RTI consistently outperforms RL baselines and enables the post-trained policy to transcend its original capability boundary, with code released at github.com/Alibaba-NLP/qqr. Agentic RL for LLMs is fundamentally limited by the on-policy reasoning bottleneck: when tasks exceed the model's current capability, successful trajectories become extremely sparse and training often collapses. RTI offers a way to escape this trap, potentially making RL post-training viable for much harder reasoning, planning, and tool-use tasks and pushing agents toward the performance of substantially stronger contemporary LLMs. The intervention trajectories are inherently off-policy, but the authors find they can still effectively guide policy optimization as long as trajectories with excessive distributional shifts are filtered out. The method relies on reward-side evidence (evaluation rubrics) during rollout, and the work is a preprint without peer review or community discussion yet.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Reinforcement learning has become a key paradigm for improving LLM agents, enabling stronger reasoning, planning, and tool-use in interactive environments. In on-policy RL methods such as PPO and GRPO, the model learns only from trajectories it generates itself, so if a task is too hard and successful trajectories are rare, the learning signal is too sparse to improve. Off-policy learning, by contrast, can use trajectories generated by other policies, but it is harder because of distribution mismatch between the behavior policy and the policy being optimized.

<a id="item-99"></a>
## [SqueezeBench: A Saturation-Resilient Math Benchmark Using Lean Proofs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13461) ⭐️ 7.0/10

SqueezeBench introduces a benchmark where each accepted submission raises the bar instead of removing a problem: every problem fixes an integer function f(n) with lower and upper records, and a submission must provide a computable Lean function with a machine-checked proof that it bounds f, is no worse than the current record at every n, and strictly better at some n. The authors release ten extremal combinatorics problems with twenty proved initial records and admission tools; frontier language model agents had 82 submissions accepted and improved every record, and two newer models added 60 more accepted submissions, improving 19 of 20 records again. Benchmark saturation is a growing problem in AI evaluation, where top models cluster at ceiling scores and benchmarks lose discriminative power; SqueezeBench offers a dynamic, proof-checked design that keeps producing harder targets as capability improves. It could benefit AI evaluation and formal verification communities by providing a continuously challenging measure of mathematical reasoning that does not require human referees. Every proof is checked by two independent Lean kernels, and the checks reject functions that compute f by exhaustive search at small n, while a maintainer only reads the submitter's report and looks for such search at larger n. For eight of the ten problems, at least one record is still asymptotically behind the best published bound, and the gap between the two records at every n shows how far certified bounds are from determining f(n).

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Lean is a proof assistant and functional programming language based on the calculus of constructions with inductive types, allowing mathematical proofs to be written and machine-checked. Benchmark saturation occurs when performance metrics hit a ceiling, limiting the ability to distinguish between new models and systems, and it is often driven by static benchmark design and data contamination. SqueezeBench addresses this by using record-based improvement on integer function bounds from extremal combinatorics, where each success sets a new target rather than removing a problem.

## [BranchOPD: Branching On-Policy Distillation Cuts Token Costs 19-31%](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13481) ⭐️ 7.0/10

BranchOPD (Branching On-Policy Distillation) selects informative decision points using student uncertainty and teacher-student disagreement, then resamples student-policy continuations from shared prefixes so that token-level teacher supervision covers both main trajectories and branches. It reduces student-generated token costs by about 19% on ALFWorld and 31% on WebShop compared with seven independent trajectories, and outperforms Vanilla OPD by 18.55 percentage points in mean mathematics accuracy (avg@16) and 13.8 points in mean ALFWorld ID/OOD success. On-policy distillation is becoming a key post-training technique for LLMs, but a single student trajectory only realizes one continuation per decision, limiting how much teacher supervision a fixed sampling budget can buy. BranchOPD shows that branching at uncertain decisions expands behavior coverage more efficiently, which could make distillation cheaper and more effective for both reasoning and agentic tasks. BranchOPD operates at token-level decisions in mathematics and within-turn decisions in agent tasks, and introduces protocol-boundary repair (PBR), which applies targeted supervision under protocol constraints to mitigate repetition and wasted generation. Selection ablations show that branching at uncertain decisions outperforms random selection and turn-initial branching.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student model on trajectories it generates itself, with a teacher model providing dense token-level supervision, combining the on-policy relevance of reinforcement learning with the dense reward signal of distillation. ALFWorld is a text-based embodied-agent benchmark for navigation and manipulation tasks, while WebShop tests whether language agents can complete real-world shopping tasks from natural-language instructions. BranchOPD addresses the coverage limitation of OPD by reusing shared prefixes to supervise multiple continuations from the same decision point.

## [HMA: Multi-Agent Session Alternation Beats Single-Agent MLE Limits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13494) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Humanize MLE Agents (HMA), which alternates two agents on a shared workspace, caps each session at k experiments, and resets context between sessions, modeled as a context-reset semi-Markov decision process (CR-SMDP). On 75 Kaggle tasks in MLE-bench, HMA achieves a state-of-the-art any-medal rate of 78.2%, exceeding the strongest open-source baseline by 8.0 points using only a quarter of its time budget. The work identifies two phenomena—long-horizon deceleration and harness staleness—that undermine current single-agent, custom-harness approaches as models improve, suggesting workflow-level alternation may be a more durable design. This could shift how the agent research community builds long-horizon ML engineering systems, emphasizing session alternation over task-specific scaffolding. The paper establishes sufficient conditions for alternation to exceed single-agent reference limits, derives an explicit experiment-budget bound, and characterizes when session caps preserve this advantage. Across six configurations spanning five heterogeneous agent pairs, HMA improves any-medal rate over constituent baselines by 6.4 points on average, and a supplementary kernel optimization case reaches 878 cycles, within 1.6% of the community leaderboard leader.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: LLM-based agents for long-horizon machine learning engineering (MLE) often rely on context engineering via custom harnesses—the runtime infrastructure around a base model that manages tools, skills, and accumulated experience. A semi-Markov decision process (SMDP) is a mathematical model for sequential decision making where the time spent in each state is a random variable, extending standard Markov decision processes. The paper's CR-SMDP adapts this formalism to model alternating agent sessions with context resets.

## [PRISM: Retrieval-Augmented Multimodal Model for Clinical Prognosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13510) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced PRISM (Population-Retrieved Inference for Structured Multimodal Prognosis), a multimodal language model that integrates structured measurements, physiological waveforms, longitudinal history, and population context for clinical prognosis. On the 1,443-task MDS-ED benchmark, PRISM achieved AUROCs of 86.2% for diagnosis, 93.1% for deterioration, 91.8% for ICU admission, and 92.8% for mortality. This work shows that structure-aware multimodal integration and retrieval in representation space can substantially improve clinical prognosis accuracy, which could affect how AI systems support emergency department decision-making. It also highlights the importance of external adaptation, as supervised fine-tuning on the external MC-MED cohort reached 0.8611 AUROC versus 0.7841 for source-only PRISM. Ablation studies show that replacing numerical EHR encoding with text serialization reduces AUROC by 3.66 points, removing pre-arrival history reduces it by 1.97 points, and zeroing retrieval reduces it by 0.99 points. Genuine retrieval outperforms random-neighbor retrieval by 0.97 macro-average points, though a paired task-level sign test does not establish a uniform advantage (p=0.067).

**Background**: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) is a technique that lets large language models retrieve and incorporate relevant information from external data sources before generating a response, reducing hallucinations and the need for retraining. AUROC is a standard metric for evaluating binary classifiers by plotting the true positive rate against the false positive rate across thresholds, and it is widely used in clinical epidemiology. The MDS-ED benchmark is an open-source framework that integrates heterogeneous static, temporal, and unstructured data modalities for scalable emergency department machine learning evaluation.

<a id="item-103"></a>
## [Causal Effect Identifiability in Causal Abstractions: A Strict Hierarchy of Criteria](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13520) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This arXiv paper formalizes causal effect identifiability directly over collections of compatible causal graphs (causal abstractions) rather than individual causal diagrams, and organizes several notions of identifiability into a strict hierarchy. It proves that a causal effect can be identified over a collection of graphs even when no single causal derivation holds uniformly across all compatible graphs. Traditional causal effect identification requires a fully specified causal graph, which is often unrealistic in complex or high-dimensional settings; this work shows identifiability can be established from partial causal information alone. It clarifies ambiguities and gaps in the existing literature, which matters for researchers in causal inference, graphical models, and AI/ML who must reason under graph uncertainty. The paper defines multiple identifiability notions at the level of graph collections and establishes a strict hierarchy among them, with the key result being that identification over a collection does not require a uniform causal derivation across all compatible graphs. As a theoretical arXiv preprint, it offers no empirical validation or accompanying code.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Causal effect identifiability asks whether the effect of intervening on one set of variables on another can be computed from observational or interventional data; classical results typically assume a fully specified causal graph. Causal abstraction addresses this by retaining only partial causal information, thereby defining equivalence classes of compatible causal graphs. A related concept is the Markov equivalence class, the set of directed acyclic graphs sharing identical conditional-independence patterns, which is what causal discovery algorithms can recover from observational data alone.

Researchers introduced MAGMA-Bench, an architecture-agnostic benchmark for evaluating persistent task reasoning in dynamic multi-robot environments, as detailed in arXiv paper 2610.13534. Alongside the benchmark, they released 261,646 task-level training examples and an architecture-agnostic expert-trace generation pipeline. Existing benchmarks separately study dynamic task execution and embodied multi-agent coordination, but rarely their intersection, so MAGMA-Bench fills a gap for measuring how agents handle execution failures, interruptions, and mission updates over long horizons. This matters for robotics and embodied AI researchers building robust planners that must coordinate heterogeneous robots in unpredictable real-world settings. MAGMA-Bench uses a structured skill-level interface that separates task-level reasoning from perception and low-level control, and it evaluates agents across increasing task horizons and controlled online perturbations. Even the strongest evaluated models fail on a substantial fraction of clean episodes, and success rates drop further under perturbations; supervised adaptation greatly improves a small model but still lags on longer and multi-robot missions.

**Background**: Long-horizon embodied agents are AI systems that must maintain task-relevant information, coordinate multiple robots with different capabilities, and adapt decisions as execution unfolds over extended periods. Vision-language-action (VLA) models are often used as low-level executors, but they struggle with long-horizon tasks because they rely mainly on current observations. Benchmarks are standardized tests that compare different system architectures fairly, and architecture-agnostic means the benchmark does not assume a specific model design.

## [New Framework Quantifies Overthinking and Maps Optimal Reasoning Termination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13570) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Step-wise Truncation Probing (STP), which repeatedly generates answers from multiple retained prefixes along a fixed reasoning trajectory to measure overthinking at the model-task level. It defines Overthinking Ratio and Accuracy Degradation, unifies dynamic routing, fixed budgets, and dynamic early exit in one framework, and builds token-accuracy Pareto frontiers plus OTBench, a benchmark that produced 70.5B reasoning tokens across six models and four benchmark groups. Overthinking wastes compute and can reduce accuracy in reasoning models, so a principled way to measure its severity and compare termination strategies could directly improve test-time scaling efficiency. The unified framework and reusable benchmark may guide future work on adaptive reasoning budgets and early-exit decoding. The reference point for each trajectory is the earliest measured position attaining maximum empirical accuracy, and Overthinking Ratio is the fraction of thinking tokens generated afterward while Accuracy Degradation is the accuracy drop from that reference to full thinking. The Pareto frontiers are constructed under a common normalized thinking-token budget, and OTBench is built from the STP process.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Reasoning models such as large reasoning LLMs generate long chains of thought (CoT) at test time, and test-time scaling means letting them think longer to improve accuracy. However, beyond a certain point additional reasoning becomes ineffective or harmful, a phenomenon called overthinking. Prior evaluations typically compare full thinking against truncated thinking using accuracy and token usage, but lack a reference for how severe overthinking is or how much a termination strategy could theoretically help.

## [MENTOR: On-Policy Pedagogical Intervention to Fix Zero-Reward Collapse in RLVR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13594) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (arXiv:2610.13594) introduces MENTOR, a multi-agent framework that performs on-policy pedagogical intervention to diagnose and recover from zero-reward collapse during Reinforcement Learning from Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) training of large reasoning models. Instead of discarding failed trajectories, MENTOR analyzes them in real time and generates targeted instructional prompts to guide the learning agent's next attempt, achieving up to 78.3% average relative gains on Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct over its base model. Zero-reward collapse is a critical training failure that can stall RLVR pipelines, which are now a key driver for improving reasoning in large language models. By turning failed trajectories into diagnostic signals and providing adaptive, forward-looking guidance, MENTOR offers a plug-and-play fix that could make RLVR training more stable and sample-efficient for the broader LLM research community. MENTOR is designed as a plug-and-play module that integrates into existing policy optimization pipelines such as GRPO without modifying the training infrastructure, and it is evaluated on math and code generation tasks. The paper reports that it outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, though as an arXiv preprint it has not yet undergone peer review.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Reinforcement Learning from Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) trains models using automatically checkable rewards, such as whether a math answer or code output is correct, rather than human feedback. This makes it scalable and less prone to reward gaming, but when task difficulty suddenly increases, all rollouts in a group may receive zero reward, producing no learning signal — a failure known as zero-reward collapse. Existing remedies often rely on static hint libraries or post-hoc refinement, which cannot adapt to the model's evolving weaknesses.

## [HOA: Multi-Session LLM Agent Framework Solves Olympiad Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13632) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced HOA (Humanize Olympiads Agent), an open, flow-level framework that coordinates multiple LLM agent sessions through three feedback channels: an independent reviewer, a structured wiki, and a Lean proof verifier. With GPT-5.6 Sol, HOA achieved perfect scores on IMO, IPhO, and IOI 2026, near-full marks on IChO 2026, solved all 672 problems on PutnamBench, and ranked first on the Lean-Eval leaderboard by solving 251 of 304 problems (82.6%). This work shows that systematic multi-session feedback can substantially close the gap between open and closed base models, as HOA with Kimi-K3 matched or exceeded the closed-source GPT-5.6 Sol + /Goal baseline on several competitions. The open release of both the model and complete inference stack addresses a key reproducibility gap left by recent high-profile systems that did not publish their full pipelines. HOA's three feedback channels target distinct failure modes: early errors propagating (independent review), missing domain knowledge (structured wiki), and apparent success masking task misinterpretation (Lean verification against trusted challenge statements). The framework is evaluated with both a closed model (GPT-5.6 Sol) and an open model (Kimi-K3), demonstrating that feedback mechanisms can bridge substantial base-model capability differences.

Reported results include a 2491-fold reduction in error against the analytical single-soliton solution, a 325-fold drop in invariant-preservation error in the selected GNLSE run with Gaussian perturbation (over 100-fold on average across repeated initializations), and a 14-fold reduction in mass drift for the KS problem.

## [Staged Curriculum Difficulty Progression in GRPO Improves Model Performance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13669) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.13669) investigates curriculum difficulty progression in GRPO, finding that persistently selecting medium-difficulty samples does not yield the strongest final model. On GSM8K, a staged easy-to-hard curriculum that progresses to a moderately harder difficulty band improves test accuracy to 0.6591 ± 0.0077 across three random seeds, compared with 0.6449 ± 0.0024 for a fixed-medium curriculum, while further progression to a harder band reduces accuracy to 0.6405 ± 0.0053. This work challenges the common assumption in GRPO that medium-difficulty samples are always optimal for online selection, showing that progressive difficulty can improve final performance. The findings are relevant to AI/ML researchers and practitioners doing reinforcement learning fine-tuning of language models, as they suggest a practical curriculum design principle for GRPO training. The paper re-estimates sample difficulty under the current policy, since difficulty changes as the policy evolves and fixed estimates can become misaligned. The benefit of staged difficulty progression is non-monotonic: progressing to a moderately harder band helps, but progressing too far to overly difficult samples hurts accuracy.

**Background**: GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization) is a reinforcement learning method used to fine-tune language models, notably popularized by DeepSeek-R1, which reduces compute requirements by using group-relative advantage estimates instead of a separate value model. Curriculum learning is a training strategy that orders samples from easy to hard, inspired by how humans learn, and has been shown to improve convergence and generalization without extra computational cost. ## [Adaptive DHiSparse Combines Speculative Decoding with Load-Adaptive KV Hierarchy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13708) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Adaptive DHiSparse introduces a load-adaptive hierarchical sparse KV cache that supports draft-model speculative decoding (DSpark and DFlash2) while dynamically adjusting how much history stays resident in GPU memory. At 100K context and concurrency 128, GLM-5.3 with DFlash2 reaches 793 output tokens/s per decode GPU, 1.45x HiSparse and 1.27x non-speculative adaptive serving, and at 500K context with concurrency 512, DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash with DSpark delivers 1.85x the output of resident serving. Long-context LLM serving is typically capacity-bound: KV memory runs out long before hardware reaches useful throughput, so this work directly attacks the bottleneck that limits batch size and concurrency. By making sparse hierarchical KV compatible with speculative decoding and adapting residency to load, it could let serving systems sustain far more concurrent long-context requests within the same latency budget. The system protects a required working set, retains extra history when GPU memory is spare, and recovers the rest via exact fused retrieval; DSpark and DFlash2 keep their original proposal and acceptance rules, with queries sharing missing-row retrieval, draft KV using a window-bounded ring, and verification staging reused across layer groups. Held to HiSparse's latency bounds, it sustains up to 4x the concurrency and 2.3-3.7x the output, and in a load step optional residency cuts host fetches from 14.2% to 1.4% of selected rows at low load while releasing memory under pressure.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Speculative decoding accelerates LLM inference by having a small draft model propose multiple tokens that the larger target model verifies in parallel, which helps because standard autoregressive generation leaves compute idle. HiSparse is a prior system that stores long-context history in host memory and fetches only the top-k selected KV entries needed for sparse attention, but it uses a fixed cache budget at every load level and does not support draft-model speculative decoding. Adaptive DHiSparse builds on HiSparse by making the GPU/host KV split adaptive to current load and by integrating DSpark and DFlash2 drafters.

## [Scoring Teachers Can't Localize Reasoning Errors, Paper Argues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13744) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.13744) tests whether the scoring signal used in on-policy self-distillation actually contains corrective credit, and finds that neither the full-distribution KL nor the token log-ratio localizes errors or pushes probability away from erroneous student tokens. The authors argue this failure is structural — locating an error in a reasoning trace is itself a search problem that a scoring teacher is never asked to solve — and propose critique-conditioned self-distillation, where a critique is generated first and then used to condition the scoring teacher. This is a rigorous negative result for a popular post-training recipe, showing that dense token-level credit from a scoring teacher may be illusory and that error localization requires explicit search rather than scoring. It has direct implications for reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) and distillation pipelines, since the proposed critique-conditioned signal also provides an advantage signal in cold-start GRPO where verifiable rewards alone give none. Using gold error spans in student rollouts, the authors show the two signals consumed by self-distillation objectives fail to distinguish correct from incorrect rollouts within the same problem; the same model with the same privileged information localizes errors substantially better when allowed to generate a critique. The resulting critique-conditioned signal is corrective within error spans and shifts probability toward backtracking tokens such as "Wait", "But", and "Actually", and the critique also helps at test time by resuming a wrong rollout with the critique in context.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: On-policy self-distillation aims to replace the sparse outcome signal of verifiable-reward RL with dense token-level credit: a teacher conditioned on the reference solution scores the student's own rollout, and the resulting distributional shift is distilled back into the policy. In knowledge distillation, the student is typically trained to match the teacher's distribution by minimizing KL divergence, which is directional and treats the teacher as the reference. Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) instead uses checkable outcomes, such as a correct final answer, as the reward signal, which is sparse and gives no per-token guidance.

Across four frontier agentic models, it achieves 1.77x the harmful path rate of the prior method STAC and 2.99x the average tool coverage. As personal agents increasingly chain tools across services like email, chat, and payment APIs, benign user requests can compose into harmful outcomes that single-tool safety checks miss. This work targets that underexplored cross-service compositional attack surface, which is directly relevant to the growing deployment of LLM-based agents and the need for robust pre-deployment safety evaluation. The framework analyzes each tool's effects, potential harms, and execution preconditions to build a directed graph capturing how one tool's effects can satisfy another's preconditions; execution feedback then diversifies successful paths via tool substitution and refines blocked preconditions in failed paths. It is an arXiv preprint (2610.13886) with no community discussion yet, and its evaluation is limited to four frontier agentic models.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Red-teaming is the practice of adversarially probing AI systems before deployment to find vulnerabilities, and for LLM agents it increasingly focuses on how agents call external tools and APIs. Personal agents are LLM-driven assistants that connect to many web and application services through tools, so their actions can accumulate and interact across service boundaries. Existing red-teaming methods typically prompt an LLM to guess harmful paths or decompose human-specified harmful goals into benign-looking action sequences, which biases generation toward familiar tool combinations and leaves novel cross-service interactions underexplored.

<a id="item-115"></a>
## [RATED: Reliability-Aware Tool Retrieval for AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13889) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose RATED, a tool retrieval framework that pairs a frozen dual-encoder with contrastive fine-tuning for semantic matching and a lightweight contextual bandit module that re-ranks candidates using real-time execution feedback. They also release EcoAgent, a benchmark of 5,172 real-world tools and over 210,000 query-tool pairs, reporting a 52.2% relative MRR improvement over the ToolLLM API retriever and a drop in fault selection rate from 86.2% to 2.9% under a 30% API failure scenario. Existing tool retrieval methods optimize only semantic relevance and ignore whether a tool is actually available at execution time, which can cause agents to repeatedly call failing APIs. By adapting to runtime failures without retraining, RATED could make AI agents more robust in production settings where external APIs are unreliable, and the EcoAgent benchmark gives the community a shared testbed for dynamic reliability evaluation. The contextual bandit re-ranks candidates based on execution feedback, enabling rapid adaptation to runtime tool failures without expensive model retraining, and the added latency is under 1 ms over encode-only inference. The work is an arXiv preprint without peer review, and the code and dataset are available at the project's GitHub repository.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: AI agents increasingly call external tools and APIs to complete tasks, so retrieving the right tool from a large toolset is a critical first step. ToolLLM is a well-known framework that collected over 16,000 real-world REST APIs and trained a tool retriever, but it focuses on semantic relevance rather than runtime availability. A contextual bandit is a reinforcement learning approach that chooses actions based on context and learns from reward feedback, making it well suited to adaptively re-ranking tools as execution results come in.

## [Wikipedia Game Benchmarks Language Models Against Humans](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13948) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.13948) uses the Wikipedia Game—navigating from one article to a target article using only internal links—to benchmark sentence embedding models and LLMs across five Wikipedia languages and five academic-literature datasets. The study constructs very large local graphs from Wikipedia and OpenAlex citations, provides open-source code, and finds that no model significantly outperforms humans, though GPT-o3 mini, GPT-4o, QwQ:32B, and DeepSeek-R1:70B achieve comparable success. This work provides a reproducible, API-independent benchmark for evaluating information retrieval and navigation capabilities of language models across languages and domains, which is important as LLMs are increasingly used to automate retrieval from large document collections. The finding that no model significantly beats humans highlights current limitations and has implications for practitioners choosing between embedding models, LLMs, and traditional methods like TF-IDF. The benchmark compares models against human and TF-IDF baselines, and performance rankings vary across languages and topics, suggesting that no single technique dominates universally. The use of local graphs built from Wikipedia and OpenAlex citations allows navigation studies without relying on third-party APIs, enabling reproducible research.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: The Wikipedia Game (also known as Wikiracing) is a game where players race to navigate from one Wikipedia article to another using only internal links, and it has been used informally to study Wikipedia's link structure. TF-IDF is a classic information retrieval weighting scheme that measures how important a word is to a document in a corpus, often used as a baseline in search and ranking tasks. Sentence embedding models represent text as vectors that encode semantic meaning, and they are commonly used for retrieval and similarity tasks. This paper applies the game as a controlled benchmark to compare such techniques with humans and LLMs.

<a id="item-117"></a>
## [IG-VLA Internalizes Future Imagination for Efficient Robot Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02626) ⭐️ 7.0/10

IG-VLA introduces a vision-language-action (VLA) framework that learns to imagine task-relevant future scene evolution directly in visual representation space, then internalizes those reasoning-derived associations into a compact Scene Gist Token. On the LIBERO-Plus Language suite, both its reasoning and gist policies outperform the strongest baseline by nearly 6% in success rate, while the gist policy achieves up to 6.38x speedup, cutting inference latency from 1081ms to 169.5ms per action chunk on a single NVIDIA A6000 GPU. This work addresses a real computational bottleneck in embodied AI: explicit future rollouts at every inference step are prohibitively expensive for real-time robot control. By showing that future spatiotemporal reasoning can be internalized into a compact token without losing accuracy, IG-VLA points toward practical, low-latency VLA deployment on robots and edge devices. The Latent Spatiotemporal Reasoning component imagines future scene evolution in visual representation space, avoiding costly pixel-level video generation, while Scene Gist Memory bypasses explicit future imagination at inference by storing scene-behavior associations in a single token. Experiments span LIBERO, LIBERO-Plus, and VLABench, with the 6.38x speedup and latency reduction measured per action chunk on a single NVIDIA A6000 GPU.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-language-action (VLA) models are multimodal foundation models that take an image observation and a text instruction and directly output low-level robot actions, a concept pioneered by Google DeepMind's RT-2 in 2023. Recent VLA research adds intermediate reasoning steps to improve manipulation, but most approaches reason only about the currently observed state rather than anticipating how the scene will evolve. Explicitly rolling out future frames at every inference step would improve foresight but introduces substantial computational overhead, which IG-VLA aims to eliminate.

## [CircuitTree Composes Phoneme Circuits into a Syllable Transcriber](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12520) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduce CircuitTree, a greedy density-scaled composition algorithm that merges arbitrarily many neural circuits into a single model, and use it to combine 39 phoneme-level circuits pruned from HuBERT into a syllable classifier. Chaining these trees into a hierarchy-of-circuits pipeline, they transcribe open syllables at 64.8% accuracy, within 1.2 points of a 66.0% ceiling that shares no circuitry, while 99.5% of circuit components are shared. This work advances mechanistic interpretability by showing that small, interpretable subnetworks can be composed into models with genuinely new capabilities, rather than only analyzed in isolation. It suggests a path toward modular, reusable neural components that could make speech and language models more transparent and efficient to build. The method overcomes the limitations of naive union composition by using a greedy, density-scaled algorithm generalized to N circuits via a Θ(N log N) tree-compose schedule. The resulting syllable classifier achieves a capability that none of the 39 individual phoneme circuits possesses on its own, though the evaluation is limited to open syllables and remains a preprint without peer review.

**Background**: Circuit discovery is a mechanistic interpretability technique that decomposes a monolithic neural network into tiny modular subnetworks, each performing an identifiable subtask. HuBERT is a self-supervised speech representation model that learns from raw audio and is widely used for tasks like automatic speech recognition. This paper asks whether such circuits can be composed to gain new abilities, using phoneme classification as a testbed for syllable transcription.

## [Language Models Can Guide Their Own Preservation via Task Banks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12602) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.12602) introduces the concept of 'self-preservation' in continual instruction tuning, where language models help choose which prior capabilities to protect. Using Task Banks of task descriptions and examples, the study finds that generated replay reduces forgetting on out-of-bank tasks, and that selection informed by the new task improves held-out retention at 4B scale. This work suggests models can partially manage their own continual learning without access to original training data, which matters for deploying frequently updated LLMs where replay data is unavailable. It also warns that average accuracy can hide specific capability losses, encouraging more careful evaluation of continual instruction tuning. Experiments with Qwen models of varying sizes show that the new task and learning rate shape which capabilities degrade, and that combining partial banks improves mean retention over either bank alone. Selection informed by the new task helps at 4B but not for smaller models, and under a fixed example budget, replacing new-task examples with replay improves preservation at the cost of smaller new-task gains.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Continual learning aims to adapt pretrained models to new tasks while retaining old abilities, but it often suffers from catastrophic forgetting, where fine-tuning overwrites prior knowledge. Replay-based methods mitigate this by mixing in old or generated data, yet they typically rely on the original training data, which may be unavailable. This paper explores whether a model can instead generate and select its own replay targets to protect its capabilities more broadly.

## [Mesh-Native Convolution Retrofits Foundation Models to Unstructured Meshes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12618) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2610.12618) introduces Mesh-Native Convolution (MNC), a locality-aware tokenisation layer that encodes unstructured mesh data analogously to CNNs. The authors show that grid-pretrained physics foundation models can be retrofitted onto arbitrary unstructured meshes with minimal retraining, sometimes achieving acceptable accuracy even in zero-shot deployment. This bridges a long-standing gap between CNN-based and GNN-based approaches in scientific machine learning, potentially allowing one physics emulator to be trained once and deployed across both structured grids and irregular meshes. It could significantly reduce training cost and improve portability for simulations involving curved boundaries or complex domains. The paper provides theoretical results confirming consistency between MNC and classical CNN encoders, and reports that the retrofitting approach outperforms existing baselines after only a few epochs of fine-tuning. It is a preprint without community discussion yet, so independent validation is still pending.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Physics foundation models aim to build versatile emulators for spatiotemporal systems by lifting physical field data into a latent representation via a tokenisation layer, typically a convolutional encoder. However, convolutional encoders assume regular grid structure, so unstructured mesh data—common in simulations with curved boundaries—usually requires graph neural networks, which are more expensive to train and encode information differently. MNC is designed to encode unstructured meshes in a CNN-like, locality-aware way, enabling portability between these two data regimes.

## [Cancerous Backdoor Attack Corrupts LLM Reasoning Gradually via Invisible Tokens](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12644) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2610.12644) introduces the 'Cancerous Backdoor Attack,' which corrupts an LLM's reasoning gradually across multiple interactions using invisible tokens and a stage controller, rather than a single trigger-response pattern. The authors report that the attack consistently produces gradual reasoning corruption across several reasoning datasets and outperforms prior backdoor attack baselines. This work shows that backdoor corruption can develop gradually and invisibly before manifesting, meaning answer accuracy alone is insufficient to detect such attacks. It raises significant security concerns for LLM deployment, especially in multi-turn conversational and reasoning-heavy applications where users cannot see hidden tokens. The attack relies on invisible tokens that inject hidden stages into the model without appearing in the user's visible request, while a stage controller tracks attack progression during inference to enable gradual activation. The abstract is truncated and the paper is an unpeer-reviewed arXiv preprint, so full technical evaluation remains limited.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

Prior research such as the BackdoorLLM benchmark has mainly studied single-point triggers in classification or text generation settings. Invisible tokens are special tokens that can be inserted into model input but are not displayed to the user, making them a stealthy attack vector.

<a id="item-122"></a>
## [Kronecker Product Attention Compresses KV Cache Up to 10x](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12657) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.12657) proposes Kronecker Product Attention (KPA), which represents key and value activations as sums of Kronecker products of compact, token-dependent matrix factors. KPA strictly generalizes Tensor Product Attention (TPA) and, in experiments across four model scales and nine benchmarks, matches or exceeds MHA, MQA, GQA, MLA, and TPA while achieving up to 10x KV cache compression. KV cache memory grows linearly with sequence length, batch size, and model depth, making it a primary bottleneck for long-context and high-throughput LLM inference. A method that cuts this memory by up to 10x without sacrificing accuracy could substantially reduce serving costs and enable longer contexts on existing hardware. KPA introduces a structured rotary embedding operator matched to the Kronecker representation that preserves RoPE's relative-position property while rotating only the compact factors, eliminating the decoupled RoPE branch used in MLA. The Kronecker head split is a tuneable hyperparameter, letting practitioners navigate the compression-accuracy trade-off at design time without architectural changes.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Autoregressive transformer inference stores past key and value tensors in a KV cache so each new token can attend to previous ones without recomputation, but this cache grows with context length. Prior methods like Multi-Query Attention (MQA), Grouped-Query Attention (GQA), and Multi-Head Latent Attention (MLA) reduce cache size through trade-offs in expressivity or added positional-encoding machinery. Tensor Product Attention (TPA) factorizes keys and values into rank-one vector outer products; KPA extends this by using full matrix factors in a Kronecker product, capturing richer block-structured patterns.

## [ReHarness Enables Backward Revision for Agentic Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12668) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proposes ReHarness, a recurrent harness framework that adds backward revision to the usual forward read and write of an agent harness, operating under a strictly online streaming protocol. After each instance, it uses the resulting trajectory as feedback to revise the reflections retrieved for that instance, propagating feedback backward through the textual harness state. Existing agent harnesses typically evolve states only through forward information flow or rely on extra replay or validation data, which limits online adaptation under distribution shifts. ReHarness shows that backward revision of textual states can improve both stability and plasticity, offering a new direction for continual learning in language model agents. ReHarness was evaluated across three continual learning benchmarks and three language model backbones, consistently outperforming raw-context baselines, memory- and reflection-based methods, and prompt optimization approaches. Further analysis indicates its revisions are source-grounded and meaningful, correcting overgeneralized or outdated experience under distribution shifts.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: An agent harness, also called agent scaffolding, is the software infrastructure surrounding a large language model that lets it act as an AI agent, and recent work suggests the harness rather than the model is a primary determinant of agent reliability at scale. Continual learning requires an agent to accumulate reusable experience across a stream of tasks and avoid interference from irrelevant experiences, but existing benchmarks struggle to evaluate this rigorously. ReHarness targets this gap by revising textual harness states backward using trajectory feedback.

## [Unsupervised Multimodal Reward Modeling via Perturbation on Frozen Qwen3-VL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12707) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces an unsupervised vision-language reward model that trains only a linear head on an intermediate layer of a frozen Qwen3-VL backbone, scoring a web caption above a perturbed version of the same caption without any human preference labels. At 32B scale, this recipe beats or matches GPT-4o on three benchmarks, including a 6.6-point lead on VL-RewardBench, using a few thousand parameters and roughly one-thousandth of fine-tuning compute. This work shows that competitive multimodal reward models can be built without expensive human preference annotations, potentially lowering the cost and complexity of aligning vision-language systems. It also reveals that benchmark rankings alone do not identify what a reward model has actually learned, which has implications for how the community evaluates and selects reward models. The choice of intermediate layer is decisive, worth 5.8 points over the last layer, and at an equal pair budget perturbations teach a frozen head 3 to 5 points more than curated RLAIF-V pairs. Because the backbone is frozen the objective is convex, and two heads trained on disjoint halves of the same perturbations tie on the benchmark yet differ by 6.4 points in hallucination judging; retraining the head on the policy's own verifier-labeled candidates cuts caption hallucination by 5.1 CHAIR points and lifts image-generation pass rate by 5.4 while scoring lower on every reward benchmark.

## [SemViews Reuses LLM Labels Across Queries with Certified Guarantees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12768) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduce SemViews, a certified semantic filter that reuses labels left behind by earlier queries as 'semantic views' to order tuples, then certifies its answer with fresh stratified oracle samples. On four workloads replayed against 725,000 recorded oracle labels, it meets both precision and recall targets on 99.63% of queries while saving 28%-45% of LLM oracle calls. LLM oracle calls dominate the cost of semantic query engines, so cutting them without sacrificing accuracy directly lowers latency and token spend for LLM-based data systems. The certified guarantee means a stale or misleading view can only cost extra calls, never correctness, which makes label reuse safe to deploy in production pipelines. The method orders tuples using signals that cost no oracle calls (prior-query labels, a local embedding, and a helper model's score), refines that order with labels it must pay for anyway, and certifies the result with fresh stratified oracle samples so targets hold with probability at least 1-δ for every workload and catalog. When a question returns in different words, views cut calls by a further 27%-36% with both targets met on at least 99.8% of such queries, whereas similarity caches and entailment-based reuse meet them on only 17%-47%.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Semantic query engines evaluate natural-language filters with an LLM, and each evaluation is a costly oracle call, so optimizers try to reduce them. Existing approaches face a dilemma: cascades that trust a cheap proxy miss precision and recall targets, methods with finite-sample guarantees save little, and all of them discard the oracle's labels once the query ends. Precision and recall are standard retrieval metrics measuring the fraction of retrieved items that are relevant and the fraction of relevant items that are retrieved, and stratified sampling divides a population into homogeneous subgroups to sample each independently.

<a id="item-126"></a>
## [REDaCT: Collapse-Free Unlearning for Large Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12769) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced REDaCT (REgenerate via Detection and Corrective Tuning), an inference-time unlearning framework for large reasoning models (LRMs) that removes forget-set information from both the chain-of-thought (CoT) and the final answer without causing reasoning collapse. REDaCT combines a binary leak detector that flags leaking sentences in the reasoning trace with an offline-learned corrective embedding that is injected at the first detected leak to steer subsequent reasoning toward non-leaking content. Existing unlearning methods for LRMs achieve forgetting at the cost of reasoning collapse, where the model can no longer produce a coherent chain-of-thought or properly terminate its reasoning. REDaCT shows that perfect answer forgetting and near-perfect CoT forgetting can be achieved while substantially preserving reasoning ability, which matters for privacy-preserving AI and regulatory compliance in models that expose their intermediate reasoning. REDaCT operates purely at inference time, requiring only an offline-learned corrective embedding rather than retraining or modifying the model's core parameters. The paper reports complete avoidance of reasoning collapse, substantially improved reasoning preservation, perfect answer forgetting, and near-perfect CoT forgetting, with code released at https://github.com/jmaccoby/redact; however, it remains an arXiv preprint without peer review or community validation yet.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Machine unlearning refers to selectively removing or reducing undesirable knowledge or behaviors from a trained model without retraining it from scratch, and it has become increasingly important for privacy and regulatory compliance in large language models. Large reasoning models extend standard LLMs by generating an explicit chain-of-thought before their final answer, so unlearning must erase forget-set information from both the reasoning trace and the answer. Prior approaches often cause reasoning collapse, a phenomenon in which multi-step reasoning degrades due to context misalignment, overreliance on memorized patterns, or resource constraints. Inference-time intervention is an established paradigm that modulates model behavior by adjusting internal activations during inference without changing trained parameters, and REDaCT applies this idea to unlearning.

## [RL-Based NAS Finds 9x Smaller Audio-Visual Speech Enhancement Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12796) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new paper introduces an RL-driven neural architecture search (RL-NAS) framework that discovers compact audio-visual speech enhancement (AVSE) architectures, evaluating only 35 candidates to find a model with 2.9 million parameters versus 22 million in the baseline. The discovered model achieves a 14.5 dB SI-SNR improvement (only 0.7 dB below baseline) while cutting model size roughly nine-fold and reducing real-time factor 4.3x on commercial mobile CPUs. This work shows that reinforcement learning-guided architecture search can make multimodal speech enhancement practical for resource-constrained edge devices such as phones and hearables, where large AVSE models are currently infeasible. It also suggests a general recipe for jointly optimizing task performance and model compactness in multimodal systems. The search space preserves temporal alignment between audio and visual streams, and a novel reward function jointly optimizes SI-SNR improvement and model compactness using proximal policy optimization (PPO) with constraint-aware action masking. Architectural analysis reveals that shallower but wider temporal convolutional blocks and aggressive visual pathway pruning maintain enhancement performance while substantially reducing computation.

**Background**: Audio-visual speech enhancement (AVSE) uses visual cues such as lip movements to help isolate a target speaker's voice from noisy audio, improving intelligibility in challenging acoustic environments. Neural architecture search (NAS) automates the design of neural networks by exploring a defined search space with a search strategy, and reinforcement learning methods like proximal policy optimization (PPO) are commonly used to guide that search. SI-SNR (scale-invariant signal-to-noise ratio) is a standard metric for measuring how much a speech enhancement model improves signal quality.

## [Generative Models as Simulators via Latent State Mapping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12847) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.12847) investigates when a generative model can act as a simulator by learning a mapping between its latent representation and a simulator's state, testing two approaches: probe-based steering and a direct inverse map. Experiments in the discrete board game Othello and a continuous world called Rayworld show that which mapping succeeds depends strongly on the environment, even though the state is decodable in every variant. If generative models can be reliably steered like simulators, they could be used for controllable world modeling, planning, and interactive content generation without explicit environment specifications. The finding that inverse maps can succeed where probe steering fails suggests a practical path for driving models by simulators they may never have learned. In Othello, editability degrades smoothly as the game is simplified, and even small rule changes have large consequences; in Rayworld, probes fail to steer along the simulator's continuous state, yet an inverse map can still set the model from that state. Both mappings are learned from observations paired with simulator states, and the model itself is trained on observations alone.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Generative models such as VAEs and diffusion models learn compact latent representations of data, but unlike simulators they offer no built-in way to read or edit the state of the world they represent. Probe-based steering uses auxiliary classifiers to find directions in latent space that control model behavior, while inverse mapping learns a direct function from simulator states back to latent codes. This paper asks whether such techniques can make a generative model behave like an editable simulator, and tests that question in both discrete and continuous environments.

## [AI Agents Struggle with Dual-Scope Pull Requests Touching Code and Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12849) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.12849) presents a causal study of 26,999 pull requests across the AIDev v5 and MOSAIC-3M datasets, covering six agent architectures plus a human baseline. Using a four-tier file taxonomy and exact 4D strata-matching, it finds that dual-scope PRs modifying both application code and deployment infrastructure make up only 4.48%–4.55% of agentic PRs but produce bimodal outcomes: reviewed ones suffer a median +13.13 hour delay (3.24x time-to-merge), while 43.1%–56.2% bypass human review entirely and merge silently in under 31 minutes. The findings expose a structural blind spot in how AI coding agents are evaluated and deployed: current benchmarks test agents in isolated sandboxes against synchronous local tests, ignoring the asynchronous CI/CD pipelines where real features ship. Because agent identity reduces the odds of human review by 63.7% even for contributors without write access, uninspected deployment changes can compromise build verification, arguing for deployment-aware benchmarks and strict path-based access controls. The study isolates integration overhead under conditional exchangeability while controlling for application volume, and reports that maintenance tasks account for 17.70%–25.41% of agent PRs, with agents acting mainly as application coders or chore workers. The protracted delays in the reviewed mode are mechanically driven by CI/CD and provisioning manifest changes, and the silent-merge mode shows a structural bypass of maintainer gatekeeping tied to agent identity.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: AI coding agents such as OpenAI Codex, Devin, GitHub Copilot, Cursor, and Claude Code now author large numbers of pull requests in real open-source repositories; the AIDev dataset alone spans nearly 1 million PRs across 116,000+ repositories. Deployment infrastructure refers to CI/CD pipelines, containers, and provisioning manifests that must change alongside application logic for a feature to ship. Causal inference techniques like stratification and matching are used to estimate treatment effects from observational data by constructing comparable treated and control groups, which this paper applies in a four-dimensional form to isolate the effect of dual-scope changes.

## [Entropy and CKA Framework Selects Optimal LLM Layers for Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12939) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.12939) proposes a principled framework for selecting multi-layer hidden states in speculative decoding, using matrix-entropy analysis to measure informativeness and centered kernel alignment (CKA) to quantify diversity. It introduces entropy- and CKA-based layer selection algorithms plus token-entropy filtering, and shows that the selected layer sets outperform fixed heuristics such as the default [2, N//2, N-3] rule in EAGLE3-style speculative decoding. Speculative decoding is a key technique for accelerating LLM inference, and the choice of which target-model layers to use strongly affects both speed and output quality. By replacing ad-hoc, position-based heuristics with a measurable, information-theoretic criterion, this work could make speculative decoding more reliable and easier to tune across different model depths and architectures. The framework analyzes layer-wise Shannon entropy, aggregate entropy under hidden-state concatenation, and pairwise CKA dynamics across depth, revealing distinct geometric and informational compartments in decoder representations. Token-entropy filtering removes hidden states associated with low predictive-entropy tokens to improve the quality of layer-selection statistics, though the approach is evaluated specifically in EAGLE3-style speculative decoding.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Speculative decoding speeds up LLM inference by having a small draft model propose several tokens that a larger target model then verifies in parallel, avoiding the sequential bottleneck of standard autoregressive generation. EAGLE3 is a state-of-the-art speculative decoding method that reuses hidden states from multiple layers of the target model, but existing implementations typically pick those layers with fixed, position-based heuristics rather than an explicit optimization. Centered kernel alignment (CKA) is a kernel-based similarity metric for comparing neural network representations, and matrix entropy is used here to quantify how much predictive information a layer's hidden states carry.

## [Evidence Anchoring Boundary Found in Multimodal LLM Visual Interventions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12981) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.12981) identifies an 'Evidence Anchoring Boundary' (EAB) in multimodal large language models, a depth threshold beyond which visual-residual edits lose their leverage. Across six checkpoints from three model families, held-out intervention effects before the EAB are 13.78x to 26.10x larger than after it, and the authors propose calibrated selective routing that improves attribute accuracy by 1.56 to 1.81 percentage points. This work offers a principled explanation for why training-free visual interventions sometimes fail or even harm correct answers, helping researchers design more reliable hallucination-mitigation strategies. It could guide layer selection and question-type-specific routing in multimodal LLM interpretability and safety research. The study applies the same visual-residual edit at successive decoder layers while matching RMS strength across depth, and finds that the edit can lose leverage before answer preferences stabilize. Counterfactual subtraction can correct a misleading preference, leave a decision unchanged, or remove a preference supporting the correct answer, so universal subtraction is neutral or harmful.

**Background**: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) combine vision and language but often hallucinate, generating content unsupported by the image. Visual interventions are training-free methods that edit internal visual representations to reduce such hallucinations, but their effectiveness varies by layer and question type. This paper systematically characterizes when these interventions retain leverage and when they help.
