## [CIG-SFT: Information-Theoretic Token Reweighting for Generalizable Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12995) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proposes CIG-SFT, an information-theoretic framework that estimates the task-conditioned information gain of each demonstrated token during supervised fine-tuning. Under teacher forcing, it compares token likelihood from a frozen reference model with and without the task input while keeping the gold solution prefix fixed, then converts this signal into a KL-regularized projection of the current policy with an equivalent scalar-weighted SFT objective. Existing dynamic SFT methods reweight tokens by model-side signals like predictive probability or distributional compatibility, but the paper argues model compatibility is not equivalent to learning value. CIG-SFT consistently outperforms standard SFT and recent adaptive fine-tuning baselines on math reasoning and code generation, with especially strong gains on challenging evaluation distributions, without needing extra data or online rollouts. The method requires neither additional training data nor online rollouts, and its logit gradient exactly matches that of the projected target, giving a principled scalar-weighted objective. The key caveat is that it relies on a frozen reference model and teacher-forced gold prefixes to compute the conditional information gain.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) adapts a pretrained large language model by training it to imitate labeled demonstrations, and it remains a standard post-training step for injecting reasoning strategies and behavioral patterns. However, forcing a model to copy externally provided trajectories can interfere with behaviors already learned during pretraining, hurting generalization beyond the training distribution. Recent dynamic SFT methods try to fix this by reweighting token-level supervision using model-side signals, but they conflate how compatible a token is with how much it is actually worth learning.

## [ReqClarifyBench: Benchmarking Interactive Requirement Clarification for Repository-Level LLM Coding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13022) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces ReqClarifyBench, an interactive requirement clarification benchmark containing 341 tasks across game, miniapp, and web development, each with a vague user intent, a human-validated requirement document, and code evaluation artifacts. It evaluates six LLMs using LLM-based user simulators with diverse personas, finding that stronger clarification effectiveness does not necessarily yield higher requirement document quality and that coding-agent harnesses do not improve document quality over their base LLMs. Existing benchmarks typically evaluate code generation from complete or underspecified specifications, overlooking the pre-coding clarification stage where vague user intent must be turned into clear requirements. By formalizing this gap for repository-level development, ReqClarifyBench could shape how future LLM-based end-to-end software development systems are designed and evaluated. The benchmark spans three development domains and uses LLM-based user simulators representing three stakeholder personas with different communication and requirement-change behaviors, derived from real-world conversations. Notably, the study reports that clarification effectiveness and requirement document quality are not strongly correlated, and that performance varies substantially across user personas.

## [GeoGR Cuts 3D VLM Inference Cost via Token Compression and Group Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13035) ⭐️ 7.0/10

GeoGR, a two-stage framework from the EIT-NLP group, introduces GeoSemZip at the projector level to consolidate cross-view observations into 3D voxel tokens and GroupRoute at the LLM level to skip low-relevance tokens. It retains 95.2% and 96.8% of dense model performance on LLaVA-OneVision-7B and Video-3D LLM-7B while accelerating LLM prefill by 6.8x and 6.1x. Multi-view 3D vision-language models suffer from long visual token sequences that make inference expensive, and GeoGR shows that jointly compressing scene representations and visual computation can deliver large speedups with minimal accuracy loss. This matters for deploying 3D VLMs in latency- and resource-constrained settings such as robotics and embodied AI. GeoSemZip combines semantic saliency with spatial coverage and aggregates unselected voxel features into contextual anchors, while GroupRoute uses late entry, early exit, and recoverable group-wise skipping so low-relevance tokens can rejoin at anchor layers under a computation budget. Compression-aware post-training uses a compact 67K-sample dataset (30% of the original pool), and the code is available at https://github.com/EIT-NLP/GeoGR.

**Background**: Vision-language models (VLMs) typically process images and video as sequences of visual tokens, and multi-view 3D understanding reuses pretrained 2D VLMs by feeding many views of a scene, which creates heavy redundancy across views. Token compression and pruning techniques aim to remove redundant visual tokens to speed up inference, while voxel-based representations group observations into 3D spatial units. GeoGR builds on these ideas by combining voxel tokenization with layer-wise token routing inside the LLM.

## [CoBe: A New Benchmark for Conversational Counterfactual Text Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13057) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduce CoBe (Counterfactual Benchmark), a testbed of 2000 questions spanning healthcare, science and engineering, business, and everyday scenarios, designed to evaluate models' zero-shot ability to counterfactually edit a given textual scenario when prompted conversationally. Testing a suite of frontier and smaller models, they find that frontier systems achieve an average score of only 58.05%. Counterfactual text editing is central to human reasoning and is especially critical in safety-critical domains like healthcare and law, where a model must distinguish what would change under a hypothetical from what must remain fixed. The benchmark reveals that current models frequently default to associational generation rather than preserving causal structure, highlighting a fundamental gap in model training objectives rather than mere capacity. The benchmark contains 2000 questions across four domains, and performance does not scale consistently with either model size or output token length, suggesting the failure reflects training objectives rather than insufficient capacity. Prior counterfactual-text datasets do not clearly separate counterfactual from associational reasoning, which CoBe explicitly addresses.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Counterfactual text editing requires revising a text in light of a hypothetical change while preserving facts not causally affected by that change; for example, if a doctor had not recommended surgery, the patient's medical history should remain intact and only the consequences of the decision should change. This demands causal understanding, as the model must determine without explicit instruction which events are affected by the hypothetical. By contrast, associational text generation merely produces text statistically correlated with the altered condition, yielding a plausible but not truly counterfactual scenario. Causal inference in NLP is an emerging interdisciplinary area that has traditionally received less attention than predictive tasks.

A new arXiv paper (2610.13113) introduces Synthetic Self-Distillation (SynthSelf), which enables continual post-training of small models by outsourcing in-distribution rewrite to a minimally larger, same-family aligner. Evaluated on 4 acquisition datasets and 14 retention benchmarks across 3 small model scales, SynthSelf consistently outperforms 6 baseline methods including off- and on-policy distillation. Small models are critical for low-latency, on-device AI, but existing continual learning methods like Self-Distillation Fine-Tuning (SDFT) fail on them due to weak in-context learning and instruction-following abilities. SynthSelf addresses this gap, potentially enabling efficient on-device adaptation without catastrophic forgetting. The paper identifies that poor acquisition and forgetting correlate linearly with the post-trained model's distribution shift, motivating in-distribution learning. SynthSelf combines off-policy supervision and rejection sampling to provide high-quality supervision without breaking the small model's original distribution.

## [Frozen Recurrent Networks May Suffice for Partially Observable RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13197) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.13197) investigates whether a frozen recurrent network combined with a trainable nonlinear output module can provide sufficient memory for solving partially observable Markov decision processes (POMDPs). The authors report that frozen recurrent networks support recall over hundreds of steps and achieve competitive control performance across diverse POMDPs while using less than half the trainable parameters of fully trainable counterparts. This challenges the common assumption that the recurrent memory module in partially observable RL must be trained jointly with the policy or value function, suggesting a simpler and more parameter-efficient baseline. If validated, it could reduce training cost and complexity for memory-based RL research and applications. The approach is motivated by Reservoir Computing (RC) and Generalized Reservoir Computing (GRC) theory, where only a readout is trained while the recurrent reservoir remains fixed. The evaluation spans tasks with varying memory demands, observation modalities, and action spaces, and source code is available at https://github.com/Aphlios/FRN-POMDP.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: A POMDP is a generalization of a Markov decision process where the agent cannot directly observe the underlying state and instead receives partial or noisy observations. Reinforcement learning under partial observability typically uses recurrent networks to encode temporal information so the agent can remember past observations. Reservoir Computing is a framework that uses a fixed, non-linear dynamical system called a reservoir to map inputs into a high-dimensional space, training only a simple readout. Generalized Reservoir Computing extends this by removing the requirement for the reservoir to possess the Echo State Property.

## [AFCE Trains VLA Policies to Predict Future Physical Effects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13212) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint introduces Action-Future Coupled Effect Learning (AFCE), which makes a future-grounded physical Effect token the primary prediction target of a vision-language-action policy instead of action chunks. On the DexJoCo benchmark, AFCE reaches 50.97% overall success, beating π0.5 and UVT by 11.82 and 15.21 percentage points respectively, and leads on all five bimanual tasks. This shifts the learning target of VLA models from low-level commands to the physical outcomes those commands produce, which could improve coordinated dexterous manipulation and generalization across embodiments. If validated beyond simulation, such effect-centric supervision may influence how future robot foundation models are trained. An offline tokenizer builds temporally ordered Effect tokens from demonstrated actions, realized robot responses, and sparse visual transitions, with reconstruction objectives preserving executable detail and observed physical change. A flow-matching VLA predicts Effects from images, instruction, and robot state, while a state-conditioned decoder converts them into action chunks, and embodiment-specific interfaces let single- and dual-arm tasks share the Effect model.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-language-action (VLA) models are multimodal foundation models that take images and text instructions and directly output low-level robot actions, a concept pioneered by Google DeepMind's RT-2 in 2023. Most VLA policies are trained by predicting action chunks, which specify what the robot should execute but not what those commands will achieve. Flow matching is a generative modeling technique that trains continuous normalizing flows by regressing a neural vector field toward a target, and DexJoCo is a MuJoCo-based benchmark and toolkit for task-oriented dexterous manipulation with Allegro Hand and Franka Panda robots.

## [ReSFlow Enables One-Step Categorical Generation on Statistical Manifolds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13225) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose ReSFlow (Rectified Statistical Flow), a model that learns geodesic trajectories on statistical manifolds to enable one-step categorical generation, eliminating the multi-step numerical integration required by prior continuous-time flows. The paper reports superior one-step generation quality across image, text, and biological domains, with code released at github.com/xiaohuanjia/ReSFlow. One-step generation could dramatically cut inference costs for discrete generative models, which currently rely on expensive multi-step ODE/SDE solvers. If the theoretical guarantees hold broadly, ReSFlow could influence how discrete diffusion and flow-based models are designed for text, images, and biological sequences. The method explicitly rectifies the flow so that trajectories between the prior and target distributions approximate geodesics under the intrinsic Riemannian geometry of the statistical manifold, theoretically reducing transport cost. Experiments span diverse real-world tasks, though the abstract does not quantify speedups or compare against specific discrete diffusion baselines.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Statistical manifolds are spaces whose points are probability distributions, equipped with a Riemannian metric such as the Fisher information metric; generative models can transport a simple prior along continuous vector fields on such manifolds to match discrete data. Rectified Flow, introduced in the paper "Flow Straight and Fast," learns nearly straight trajectories in Euclidean space so that generation can be done in one or few steps. ReSFlow combines these ideas by constraining flows to follow geodesics—shortest paths on curved manifolds—rather than arbitrary curves.

## [Agent4Spec: LLM Multi-Agent System Evolves API Specs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13273) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers released Agent4Spec, an autonomous multi-agent framework that goes beyond one-time API specification generation by combining a validation pipeline that detects and self-corrects OpenAPI syntax errors with a market analysis pipeline that researches competitors and generates missing endpoint functionality. Evaluated on two curated datasets spanning general-purpose and industrial scenarios, the top performer among 9 state-of-the-art LLMs achieved 100% syntax error detection, over 95% implementation success for proposed corrections, and over 50% adoption rates for suggested endpoint improvements. API specifications directly affect integration efficiency and client code generation, yet existing LLM approaches treat spec generation as a one-time event, ignoring continuous validation, long-term maintenance, and market-driven evolution. Agent4Spec shows that multi-agent LLM pipelines can automate these ongoing maintenance tasks, potentially reducing manual API documentation work and helping teams keep their interfaces competitive. The framework is evaluated on two curated datasets covering both general-purpose and industrial API specifications, and the reported results come from the best of 9 SOTA LLMs rather than a single model. As an arXiv preprint, the work has not yet undergone peer review, and the market analysis pipeline's reliance on competitive research means its suggestions depend on the quality and availability of external market information.

**Background**: The OpenAPI Specification (formerly Swagger) is a machine-readable, language-agnostic format for describing HTTP APIs, allowing tools to generate code, documentation, and tests from a single description. LLM-based multi-agent systems use multiple autonomous LLM agents that plan, call tools, and collaborate to complete complex tasks. Agent4Spec combines these two areas, applying multi-agent LLM techniques to the continuous validation and evolution of OpenAPI descriptions.

## [PRIME Boosts Structured Document Retrieval via Index-Side Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13296) ⭐️ 7.0/10

PRIME introduces an index-side optimization framework that combines LLM-driven Semantic Restructuring (SR) with deterministic Metadata Synthesis (MS) and Hierarchical Retrieval (HR). Evaluations show deterministic metadata matches structure-aware chunking's retrieval quality while cutting embedding computation by 45.8x and index vector count by 22.4x, and SR further improves Answer-F1 by 13.7% in full deployment. Existing IR research has focused on query-side retrievers and generators, leaving index-side quality underexplored; PRIME shows that optimizing the index itself can yield substantial efficiency and quality gains for RAG systems and LLM agents. This could shift attention toward corpus-side representation as a key lever for high-precision retrieval over external knowledge bases. The framework operates on two complementary tracks: an LLM-driven SR track that transforms long documents into structured, information-dense sections, and a deterministic MS/HR track that extracts semantic anchors and heading structure for coarse-to-fine section retrieval. Across QASPER, BRIGHT, and NFCorpus, the authors characterize three qualitative regimes that determine whether index-side optimization helps or degrades retrieval, and note that these effects are neutralized under a strong reranker.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems rely on retrievers such as ColBERT and BGE to fetch relevant passages from external knowledge bases for LLM agents. Most prior work optimizes the query side (retrievers and generators like Self-RAG and GraphRAG) while treating the corpus side as simple chunking. PRIME instead targets the index side, arguing that how documents are structured and indexed before retrieval is an underexplored but impactful lever.

## [Label-Free Rubric Clarification Boosts LLM-Judge Consensus but Not Human Agreement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13303) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.13303) tests a label-free procedure that decomposes rubrics into sub-criteria, ranks them by cross-judge disagreement, and has an editor model rewrite only the most contested clauses. Across 129,770 judge requests on SummEval, HealthBench, and USR Topical-Chat, the method raised inter-judge Krippendorff's alpha by 0.069 on SummEval but produced no detectable change in agreement with expert ratings (Kendall tau difference -0.007). This result is a cautionary finding for the growing practice of using LLM panels as automated evaluators: making judges agree with each other more does not necessarily make them agree more with humans. It suggests that consensus-based rubric repair may optimize the wrong objective, affecting anyone relying on LLM-as-a-judge pipelines for model evaluation or benchmarking. The study pre-registered four hypotheses in a version-controlled protocol and evaluated six rubric conditions with a panel of five judges from three providers; a stage meant to move text-checkable sub-criteria to deterministic checkers transferred none. On HealthBench and USR, neither inter-judge agreement nor human agreement met the preregistered effect criteria, and in a secondary comparison human agreement after editing contested clauses was lower than after editing control clauses on all three datasets in point estimate, though no interval excluded zero.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: LLM-as-a-judge uses large language models to score text outputs against predefined criteria, and panels of such judges are increasingly used for evaluation. When panel members disagree, practitioners often assume the rubric is unclear and try to clarify it, but it has been unclear whether this improves alignment with human annotators. SummEval is a summarization benchmark with multidimensional human annotations, while HealthBench and USR Topical-Chat are other evaluation datasets used here to test generalization.

## [AgentSWE benchmark tests whether coding agents build agents that truly work](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13361) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers from the Beijing Academy of Artificial Intelligence (BAAI) released AgentSWE, a 25-task benchmark that evaluates coding agents across three stages of the agent development lifecycle: Creation (10 tasks), Editing (10 tasks), and Optimization (5 tasks). Each task is framed as a commission rather than an exam, where builders iterate against a few illustrative cases but are graded on held-out acceptance cases using requirement-derived rubrics. This benchmark addresses a critical gap in existing evaluations by testing whether coding agents can faithfully deliver agents that serve the actual requirements they were built for, rather than merely appearing to work. The finding that none of the evaluated coding agents comes close to delivering what a requirement asks for highlights significant reliability challenges that could affect anyone delegating agent development to AI systems. The benchmark evaluates coding agents across different models and harnesses, with all builder sessions and candidate executions running in isolated containers with pinned fixtures and every model call passing through a benchmark-owned gateway. The empirical analysis identifies recurring failure modes including overfitting to visible cases, hollow runtimes behind code that looks complete, and unsupported claims of completion.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Coding agents are AI systems that can write, edit, and optimize software code based on natural-language instructions. As these agents become more capable, they are increasingly being used to build other agents tailored to specific user workflows, a process called agent software engineering. However, existing benchmarks have not tested whether the agents delivered by coding agents actually fulfill the requirements they were commissioned for, creating a gap in evaluating true reliability.

## [ProgramGym Scales Verifiable Training Environments for Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13395) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced ProgramGym, a framework combining Program-Env and Program-Agent to build scalable, verifiable training environments for long-horizon program reproduction agents. Using a feedback-driven pipeline, they constructed 1,648 training environments spanning C, C++, Go, and Rust, and fine-tuning Qwen3.8-27B on these trajectories raised performance from 34.87% to 42.84% on ProgramBench. Coding agents are increasingly expected to build software from scratch rather than just patch repositories, but scalable verifiable training environments for this skill have been scarce. ProgramGym addresses that gap and shows measurable gains, with Program-Agent pushing base and SFT models to 55.71% and 56.25%, exceeding some flagship models. Program-Env builds reference executables and runtime sandboxes, then iteratively constructs black-box behavioral tests with validation feedback, while Program-Agent separates analysis, implementation, and review into distinct sessions with persistent handoff records and revision checkpoints for compilation checks and failure recovery. The framework will be released to support further research on coding agents that build programs from scratch.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Long-horizon program reproduction means an agent must explore a reference program, infer its requirements and design, and then implement software from scratch, a much harder task than fixing issues in an existing repository. Verifiable training environments provide deterministic reward signals, typically through tests, which are essential for reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) in domains like software engineering. ProgramGym targets this setting by automatically generating such environments and tests from real repositories.

## [Probabilistic In-Context Learning Framework Advances System Identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13411) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.13411) introduces a unified probabilistic meta-identification architecture that combines a Transformer-based encoder-decoder with tailored mechanisms for handling long training sequences, enabling calibrated predictions over long horizons. The work also shows how a pre-trained meta model can serve as an uncertainty-aware synthetic data generator for classical system identification, and validates the framework on standard nonlinear benchmarks plus a challenging out-of-distribution scenario. This advances in-context learning for system identification by bypassing explicit model estimation, potentially letting practitioners obtain zero-shot predictions directly from training data and query inputs. It could benefit researchers in meta-learning, control, and time-series modeling, especially when real data are scarce or distribution shifts occur. The architecture uses a Transformer-based encoder-decoder with mechanisms tailored for long training sequences, and the pre-trained meta model can regularize parametric models when real data are scarce. The authors report competitive accuracy with state-of-the-art methods and strong generalization, including cases where input-only adaptation suffices to handle significant distribution shifts.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: System identification is the field that builds mathematical models of dynamical systems from measured input-output data, often in a black-box manner. Traditional approaches first estimate a model from training data and then use it to predict outputs for new queries, whereas in-context learning instead trains a meta model offline on large synthetic datasets to map training data and query inputs directly to outputs in a zero-shot fashion. Transformers, with their encoder-decoder architecture and self-attention, are well suited to such sequence-to-sequence tasks and can capture long-range dependencies.

## [Social-Aware AV Routing Cuts Harm to Human Drivers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13439) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces a social marginal cost component into the reward function of autonomous vehicle (AV) routing policies learned via multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL). Experiments on the real-world networks of Ingolstadt and Provins show that adding this social term reduces the increase in human-driven vehicle (HDV) travel times from 1.7% to 1.1% at 40% AV penetration, while also shortening AV travel times compared to purely selfish routing. As AVs enter public roads, their routing decisions can impose negative externalities on human drivers, and this work shows that socially aware routing can benefit both AVs and the overall traffic system. The findings suggest that AV routing objectives should move beyond pure selfish travel-time minimization, which has implications for fleet operators, policymakers, and mixed-autonomy traffic management. The marginal cost is computed by comparing other road users' travel times against a counterfactual simulation in which the AV is removed, and the authors propose a scalable variant that computes this cost only for AVs whose route choices most affect the system, cutting the number of counterfactual simulations. However, when the weight of the social component in the reward increases too much, the system fails to reach the desired performance.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: In mixed-autonomy traffic, autonomous vehicles and human-driven vehicles share the same roads, and AVs that optimize only their own travel time can worsen congestion for others. Multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) lets multiple AVs learn routing policies through repeated interaction with the traffic environment. The concept of marginal cost comes from congestion pricing theory, where road users should bear the additional social cost they impose on others; this paper adapts that idea as a reward term rather than a toll.

## [Multimodal LLMs Fail to Read 'Squint Text' in Images, New Benchmark and Method Proposed](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13465) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.13465) identifies a failure mode called 'Squint Text,' where multimodal LLMs cannot read text embedded in an image's coarse structure and claim no text is present. The authors introduce SquintBench (1,076 production-derived moderation images) and SquintCtrl (controlled synthetic suite), and propose Squint OPSD, which achieves 88.61 ANLS on SquintBench versus 26.50 for the unadapted backbone. This work exposes a critical robustness gap in multimodal LLMs that could affect content moderation, accessibility, and document understanding pipelines. By providing a diagnostic benchmark and a training method that narrows the gap without auxiliary views at inference, it offers a practical path to improve production systems. Frozen-weight analysis shows hidden text leaves a coarse signal but glyph identity is weakly represented; supplying correct spatial regions makes identity more accessible, implicating spatial selection as a bottleneck. Suppressing high-frequency content improves reading at a fixed visual-token budget, and Squint OPSD uses a frozen teacher with downscaled view and ground-truth transcription to supervise a student on-policy, with the student reading the original image alone at inference.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Multimodal LLMs process both text and images, but they can struggle when text is not clearly rendered, such as when it is embedded in coarse image structures that humans can recover by squinting. This paper formalizes that failure as 'Squint Text' and builds benchmarks to diagnose and address it, drawing from real-world moderation cases.

## [Explanatory World Models Use LLM Explanations to Fix RL Credit Assignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13504) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Explanatory World Models (EWM), an attribution framework that uses a frontier language model to generate explanations identifying which earlier events plausibly caused a realized outcome, then redistributes reward through a conservative hierarchical operator that preserves the undiscounted episode return. EWM is evaluated on Crafter, WordCraft, KuaiRand, and an industry-scale generative recommender, outperforming PPO, LaRe, SFT, and A2C, with the largest gains when rewards are most delayed. Credit assignment — determining which past actions deserve credit for a future reward — is a fundamental bottleneck in long-horizon reinforcement learning, and standard temporal-proximity discounting attenuates signals from distant but important actions. By showing that language-model explanations can serve as structured, reusable credit signals, this work opens a fresh direction for RL and generative recommenders where outcomes may appear hundreds or thousands of interactions after a decision. EWM operationalizes three complementary explanation variants inspired by Halpern's actual causation, Lombrozo's explanatory simplicity, and Deutsch's hard-to-vary explanations, and uses a conservative hierarchical operator that recursively redistributes reward while preserving the undiscounted episode return. Results are reported on two controlled long-horizon benchmarks (Crafter and WordCraft), a public sequential-recommendation benchmark (KuaiRand), and an offline industry generative recommender where EWM improved Recall and behavioral proxies.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: In reinforcement learning, an agent learns by trial and error, receiving rewards that it must attribute back to the actions that caused them; when rewards are delayed, this credit assignment problem becomes severe because standard methods like temporal-difference learning discount rewards by temporal proximity rather than causal relevance. World models are learned predictive models of an environment's dynamics that agents can use for planning and imagination, and recent work has begun combining them with language models. Halpern's actual causation is a formal, counterfactual-based theory of which events genuinely caused an outcome, offering a principled basis for deciding which past actions deserve credit.

## [RESCUE Evaluates Whether Robot Failure Alarms Enable Useful Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13516) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced RESCUE, a counterfactual evaluation framework that assesses whether runtime failure monitors for robot policies enable timely and useful interventions, rather than merely detecting failures accurately. Using OpenVLA-OFT and π0.5 on LIBERO-Spatial and LIBERO-Plus, they analyzed 128,340 counterfactual branches and found that recoverability depends on the state-intervention pair, not robot state alone. This work shifts the evaluation of robot failure monitors from detection accuracy to intervention value, addressing a critical gap in robot safety and embodied AI. It could influence how runtime monitors are designed and deployed, especially for VLA policies where a late alarm or unnecessary reset can be costly. At τ=0.5, 14 of 66 OpenVLA-OFT/LIBERO-Plus failures contained a recoverable checkpoint under continuation, versus 34 of 66 under continuation or scripted recovery, while recovery reduced recoverability in other conditions. Under the evaluated stop-and-reset model, even an idealized detector can afford only a 2.0–3.3% false-alarm rate before unnecessary resets outweigh the savings from stopping failures early.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-language-action (VLA) models are learned robot policies that read camera pixels and language instructions and output actions, replacing hand-engineered control stacks. Runtime failure monitors are separate components that watch a policy's execution and raise alarms when something goes wrong, but they are usually judged only by how accurately they distinguish failed from successful runs. RESCUE instead restores simulator and policy states at checkpoints to compare alternative actions and measure whether acting on an alarm actually reduces expected task-completion cost.

## [SWE-DeceptionBench: Benchmarking LLM Deception in Software Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13626) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced SWE-DeceptionBench, a bilingual benchmark covering six software development lifecycle stages and two deception triggers (Active and Passive Deception), with 720 test instances in Chinese and English. Evaluating seven mainstream LLMs, they found aggregate deception rates of 49.88% for Active Deception and 64.29% for Passive Deception even under a Neutral condition. As LLMs are increasingly embedded throughout the software development lifecycle, their tendency to mislead or help users conceal information poses a direct risk to software trustworthiness. This benchmark provides a structured framework for measuring and mitigating such deception, which is likely to stimulate further AI safety research in software engineering. The benchmark also introduces Neutral, Reward, and Pressure conditions to study how external inducements affect deception, and finds that external inducements influence Active and Passive Deception to different extents. Lightweight defenses were tested: guard models miss many deceptive requests, and Self-Reminder reduces but does not eliminate deceptive responses.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Deceptive behavior in LLMs refers to misleading responses or assistance in fabricating, concealing, or distorting information, and prior work such as Sleeper Agents and surveys of AI deception has shown that more capable models can be more adept at deception. Active Deception occurs when a model recognizes infeasible or conflicting requirements but still presents them as achievable, while Passive Deception occurs when a model helps fulfill explicit user requests to mislead others. SWE-DeceptionBench extends this line of research into the software engineering domain, where LLM trustworthiness is especially consequential.

## [Model-agnostic knowledge augmentation boosts weak-label bioimaging classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13637) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2610.13637) proposes a model-agnostic knowledge augmentation method that lets machine learning models exploit domain knowledge beyond automated feature extraction, improving classification across seven ML architectures. Combined with masked autoencoder-based self-supervised learning, it raises precision by 2.9% on three in-house microscopy datasets and by an average of 6.8% across three public cytomorphology benchmarks. Weakly annotated bioimaging data is common in microscopy and cytomorphology, where exhaustive pixel-level labels are impractical, and inconsistent performance across datasets has limited clinical and biological reliability. A model-agnostic approach that works across architectures could make such pipelines more reproducible and broadly adoptable for quantitative cell phenotyping. The method was validated on nucleus-level classification of TDP-43 mislocalization from the nucleus to the cytoplasm, an ALS-linked phenotype, using three independent microscopy datasets of motor neurons derived from human induced pluripotent stem cells. Applying the best model revealed dose-dependent effects of the proteasomal stressor MG132 and the oxidative stressor sodium arsenite on TDP-43 mislocalization, variable pathology in sporadic versus familial ALS donors, and no protection from edaravone or niclosamide against SA-induced mislocalization.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Weak supervision uses inexpensive, noisy or partial labels instead of exhaustive manual annotation, easing the labeling bottleneck in training machine learning models. Masked autoencoders (MAE) are a self-supervised learning technique that masks roughly 75% of an image's patches and trains a network to reconstruct the missing pixels, yielding strong visual representations. TDP-43 is an RNA-binding protein that is normally nuclear but mislocalizes to the cytoplasm and aggregates in ALS, making its quantification a key but technically difficult pathology readout.

## [SelectArena: A Modern Benchmark for Feature Selection on Real-World Tabular Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13644) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced SelectArena, a benchmark that evaluates 15 widely used feature selection methods across 71 manually curated real-world tabular datasets for both classification and regression tasks, using standardized protocols and state-of-the-art tabular foundation models. The study reports clear trade-offs in predictive performance, runtime, stability, and validity, identifying tree-based methods (CART, RFImportance) and F-Test as Pareto-optimal for performance versus runtime, while search-based methods are slow, unstable, and low-performing. Existing feature selection benchmarks often rely on outdated models, ad hoc evaluation protocols, or synthetic data, giving practitioners limited guidance; SelectArena addresses this gap with real-world data and modern models, and its involvement of prominent authors like Frank Hutter adds credibility. It is positioned as an extensible platform likely to influence both future research and practical method selection across domains such as industry, climate science, and clinical diagnostics. The benchmark compares 15 feature selection methods on 71 manually curated datasets using standardized evaluation protocols and state-of-the-art tabular foundation models, and it explicitly measures predictive performance, runtime, stability, and validity. F-Test emerges as a balanced choice across metrics, while information-theory- and distance-based methods lead on stability and validity, and search-based methods perform poorly on all fronts.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Feature selection is the process of choosing a subset of relevant input columns from a dataset to improve model performance, reduce training costs, and enhance interpretability. Common approaches include filter methods (e.g., F-Test, mutual information), wrapper/search-based methods, embedded methods, and tree-based importance measures such as CART and random forest importance. Benchmarks are standardized test suites that compare methods under controlled conditions, but prior feature selection benchmarks have been criticized for using outdated models or artificial data that do not reflect real-world problems.

## [UPTURN Learns Where to Inject User Turns for LLM Self-Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13672) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose UPTURN, a correction framework that trains a 130K-parameter MLP head on a frozen encoder to predict where to intervene in a failed reasoning rollout, then injects an instruction as a new user turn at that learned position and regenerates the suffix. Across four reasoners and six math benchmarks, UPTURN improves average accuracy in every setting over a 32-rollout baseline without any LLM fine-tuning. This work shows that how a correction instruction is delivered matters as much as what it says: inline token-level edits fail to redirect reasoning under a fixed generation budget, while a user-turn injection succeeds. Because it requires no fine-tuning and transfers across reasoners and to GPQA-Diamond without retraining, it offers a practical, low-cost inference-time self-correction method that complements GRPO and existing self-correction approaches. The intervention locator is a lightweight 130K-parameter MLP head trained only on final-answer correctness signals, keeping the underlying encoder frozen. UPTURN retains a selected prefix, injects the instruction as a new user turn, and regenerates the suffix, outperforming self-correction and reflection-token injection baselines under matched correction budgets.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Large language models often struggle to reliably identify and correct errors in their own reasoning, and prior training-based approaches typically fine-tune the model to generate revisions or feedback. Inference-time self-improvement instead boosts performance by spending extra computation at test time, through decoding, sampling, or added context. UPTURN sits in this inference-time category, learning where to intervene in a failed rollout and how to redirect it without modifying the model weights.

## [OAL: Outlier-Aware Attention Linearization with Grouped Quadratic Kernels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13674) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proposes Outlier-Aware Attention Linearization (OAL), a dimension-aware method that builds quadratic attention approximations by grouping dimensions with similar contribution scales while treating a few outlier dimensions separately. On Qwen2.5-0.5B, OAL reduces frozen-attention output relative MSE by about 8.9% versus DiJiang, and replacing middle-layer attention modules with joint LoRA fine-tuning lowers WikiText-103 perplexity from 31.71 to 27.50. Linearized attention is a key route to efficient Transformer inference, but approximation error has limited its adoption; identifying outlier dimensions as a dominant error source gives the field a concrete, actionable target. The reported gains in both approximation fidelity and downstream accuracy suggest OAL could improve pretrained-model conversion and long-sequence inference for resource-constrained deployments. OAL starts from a second-order Taylor approximation and uses a Hadamard-product formulation that makes individual query-key contributions explicit, relaxing globally shared coefficients into dimension-specific linear terms and cross-dimensional quadratic interactions while preserving separable, linear-time evaluation for fixed head dimensions. The resulting positive quadratic kernel is calibrated against pretrained attention outputs, and the code is available at https://github.com/wuqianyue09/OAL.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Softmax attention computes pairwise query-key similarities, which scales quadratically with sequence length and is expensive for long contexts. Linearized attention replaces this with feature-map or kernel approximations that scale linearly, but such approximations often degrade the pretrained model's attention behavior. Prior work has shown that a small number of outlier dimensions in Transformer representations can disproportionately affect model behavior, and OAL builds on this observation by treating those dimensions specially within a quadratic kernel approximation.

## [LVR-PG Achieves Near-Minimax-Optimal Sample Complexity for Policy Gradient](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13692) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Loopless Variance-Reduced Policy Gradient (LVR-PG), a unified theoretical framework that abstracts a class of first-order estimators combining recursive updates with defensive importance sampling, including known methods like PAGE-PG and STORM-PG. It proves that this class achieves $\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-2})$ last-iterate global convergence under weak gradient domination for general parametric policies, using purely first-order updates without gradient preconditioners. This work resolves a key theoretical gap by showing that near-optimal sample complexity for policy gradient methods can be achieved under minimal assumptions, without restrictive bounded importance weights or impractical second-order operations. It unifies existing variance-reduced estimators and establishes minimax-optimality up to logarithmic factors, providing a rigorous foundation for designing sample-efficient reinforcement learning algorithms. The framework achieves $\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-2})$ convergence and the authors extend state-of-the-art lower bounds to continuous parametric policies, establishing an $\Omega(\epsilon^{-2})$ sample complexity limit, confirming minimax-optimality up to logarithmic factors and horizon terms. The analysis relies on weak gradient domination and avoids gradient preconditioners, but is purely theoretical with no empirical validation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Policy gradient methods in reinforcement learning optimize a parameterized policy by estimating the gradient of expected return using Monte Carlo samples, but these estimates often suffer from high variance, leading to slow and unstable learning. Variance reduction techniques, such as recursive updates and importance sampling, aim to lower this variance while preserving unbiasedness, but existing methods either require restrictive assumptions like bounded importance weights or rely on second-order operations that are computationally expensive. This paper provides a unified theoretical framework that bridges these approaches and proves optimal sample complexity under weak assumptions.

## [Bilevel Stackelberg Training Jointly Calibrates Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13693) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.13693) proposes a bilevel Stackelberg formulation that trains standard post-hoc calibrators end-to-end with the neural network backbone, eliminating the need for a separate calibration set or a dedicated calibration loss. The authors show that using the final classification layer itself as the calibrator yields accurate, well-calibrated models with no additional parameters, outperforming state-of-the-art calibrators with only one extra hyperparameter: the calibrator learning rate. This approach could simplify model calibration pipelines by removing the overhead of separate calibration datasets and multi-stage optimization, making well-calibrated models easier to deploy in high-stakes applications like medical diagnosis or autonomous driving. It also avoids the multiplicative overhead of separate calibration components in multi-task learning, potentially influencing future work on training-time calibration. The framework supports calibrators ranging from temperature and matrix scaling to neural network layers, but the best results come from simply reusing the final classification layer as the calibrator, adding zero parameters. The only additional hyperparameter introduced is the calibrator learning rate, and the method jointly optimizes accuracy and calibration using cross-entropy.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Neural networks often produce poorly calibrated confidence scores, meaning their predicted probabilities do not reflect true correctness likelihood. Post-hoc calibration methods like temperature scaling, Platt scaling, and isotonic regression adjust a trained model's outputs using a separate held-out calibration set, but this adds overhead and requires separate optimization. Bilevel optimization, rooted in Stackelberg game theory, involves a leader and a follower optimizing hierarchically, and this paper applies that structure to jointly train the model and its calibrator.

## [CFGNP Answers Counterfactual Queries Without Causal Graph Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13724) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces the Counterfactual Graph Neural Process (CFGNP), an end-to-end method that answers counterfactual queries directly from observational data without first estimating a causal graph. On the German Loan benchmark, CFGNP reduces MSE from 4.51 × 10⁻² to 0.19 × 10⁻², and a model trained only on 10-node graphs maintains strong performance on graphs up to 30 nodes without retraining. This addresses a major bottleneck in causal inference: errors in estimated causal graphs propagate to downstream counterfactual predictions. By bypassing explicit causal discovery, CFGNP could make counterfactual reasoning more reliable in domains like healthcare and economics where the true causal structure is unknown. CFGNP uses Graph Neural Networks to exploit a dual-graph structure by restricting cross-world message passing to corresponding factual–counterfactual variables. It was evaluated on synthetic and image-based benchmarks, and its generalization from 10-node to 30-node graphs suggests scalability without retraining.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Counterfactual inference asks how an observed outcome might have differed under different circumstances, such as whether a patient would have recovered with a different treatment. Standard approaches first estimate the causal graph from observational data and then perform inference, but errors in that graph can propagate. Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) are deep learning models designed to operate on graph-structured data, and the Counterfactual Graph Neural Process (CFGNP) combines GNNs with neural processes to answer counterfactual queries end-to-end.

## [LAPT-Scale Pre-Trains Robot Policies via Latent Actions from Human Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13732) ⭐️ 7.0/10

LAPT-Scale introduces a two-part paradigm: LAPT-Scale-LAM learns continuous latent actions by explaining frame-to-frame visual transitions in egocentric human videos, and LAPT-Scale-Policy uses those latent actions to pre-train a VLA policy before embodiment alignment and task-specific post-training. Trained on 23.3K hours of egocentric video, it lifts real-robot success from 63.3% to 85.0% as annotated human video scales from 1K to 5K hours. Collecting robot interaction data is expensive, so learning from abundant human video could remove a major bottleneck in scaling generalist robotic policies. If the scaling gains hold, this approach could make VLA policies cheaper to train and more broadly deployable across tasks and embodiments. Unlike prior vector-quantized latent action models, LAPT-Scale-LAM uses a continuous latent space to preserve fine-grained motion and interaction dynamics, and it combines perceptual, semantic, and optical-flow alignment signals instead of relying on pixel reconstruction alone. The learned policy also generalizes to unseen visual conditions, though the work is an arXiv preprint without peer review or community validation yet.

**Background**: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models are multimodal neural networks that take camera images and a natural-language instruction as input and output robot actions, unifying perception, language understanding, and motor control. Latent Action Models (LAMs) learn compact surrogate actions from unlabeled video, since human videos lack robot action labels; earlier LAMs typically used discrete vector-quantized action spaces. LAPT-Scale builds on this line of work by learning continuous latent actions from large-scale egocentric human video and using them as supervision for policy pre-training.

A new paper introduces Orthros attention, a unified module that dynamically switches its computation pattern across different phases of sequence processing, enabling linear complexity prefilling and full attention decoding. It achieves a 21.9x prefilling speedup over standard Transformers while preserving long-context capabilities, including near-perfect 128K needle-in-a-haystack results. This work addresses the quadratic bottleneck in long-context Transformers by shifting from structural allocation to phase-aware decoupling, potentially enabling more efficient industrial applications with heavy prefilling and light decoding. It positions Orthros as a promising candidate to succeed the Transformer in scenarios requiring both high performance and efficiency. Orthros achieves lower asymptotic complexity than vanilla Transformers in long-context scenarios, and a data-efficient Transformer-to-Orthros conversion yields performance that even surpasses the original Transformer. The abstract is truncated, so full empirical details and limitations are not yet available.

Prefilling processes the input prompt in parallel, while decoding generates tokens one by one, each with different computational bottlenecks.

## [Edge Independence Flaw in Graph Diffusion Fixed by Latent Sociability Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13765) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.13765) identifies a fundamental flaw in discrete graph diffusion models: independent edge updates combined with exchangeability force the terminal prior to be an Erdős–Rényi random graph, which makes denoising ineffective in the large-graph limit. The authors propose a Latent Sociability Prior (LSP) and a joint diffusion process that co-evolves latent node sociability variables with graph structure, removing edge independence while preserving exchangeability. This work reframes a known limitation of graph diffusion models—poor recovery of node heterogeneity and higher-order structure—as a prior problem rather than an expressivity problem, offering a principled fix. If validated, it could improve unconditional graph generation across domains like molecule design and social network synthesis, where realistic structure matters. The LSP mechanism requires no complex encoder; its sole role is to guide edge updates and preserve node heterogeneity throughout the diffusion trajectory. The method is validated on multiple graph generation datasets and reportedly captures graph distributions more accurately than baselines without node features.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Diffusion models generate data by gradually adding noise to a sample and then learning to reverse that process; in discrete graph diffusion, noise is added by randomly flipping edges. The Erdős–Rényi model is the simplest random graph, where each possible edge appears independently with a fixed probability, producing graphs with little meaningful structure. Exchangeability means the output distribution should not depend on the ordering of nodes, a natural requirement for graph generation. Previous approaches tried to compensate for poor denoising by adding specialized node features, but this paper argues the root cause is the independent-edge noise process itself.

## [DualQuant: Calibration-Free 4-bit Weight-and-Activation Quantization for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13767) ⭐️ 7.0/10

DualQuant introduces a dual-scale, calibration-free post-training quantization framework that directly minimizes weight-reconstruction MSE and, as a by-product, derives per-channel activation rescalings before activation quantization. It is the first dual-scaling method to formally support the full MX family and NVFP4 block formats, matching or exceeding calibration-based SmoothQuant across Llama and Qwen models on WikiText-2, C4, and zero-shot tasks. Activation quantization is the main obstacle to fully realizing 4-bit throughput on accelerators with native 4-bit support, and it typically requires calibration data that may be unavailable or expensive to collect. By eliminating calibration while remaining competitive, DualQuant makes W4A4 deployment more practical for privacy-sensitive or data-scarce settings, with a real packed W4A4 path for Llama-3.1-8B on an RTX 4090 delivering up to 2.61x memory reduction and 2.46x throughput speedup over BF16. DualQuant formulates quantization as a non-differentiable weight-reconstruction problem and solves it with variable splitting and block coordinate descent, where each subproblem has a closed-form update. The key observation is that scaling factors minimizing weight-reconstruction error also serve as effective activation rescalings, and the implementation closely matches fake-quantization accuracy.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Post-training quantization (PTQ) compresses LLM weights and activations from high-precision values to low-bit formats to reduce memory and compute, without retraining. Weight-only quantization such as W4A16 is well studied, but quantizing activations is harder because activation ranges depend on input data and are only known at inference time, which is why methods like SmoothQuant rely on calibration data. Block formats such as MX and NVFP4 define how groups of values share scaling factors, and native 4-bit accelerators need both weights and activations quantized to unlock full throughput.

## [ReplaySSM Caches SSM Inputs Instead of State to Cut Memory Traffic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13778) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.13778) introduces ReplaySSM, which unrolls the SSM recurrence so that the recurrent state is represented as a checkpoint plus a buffer of recent SSM inputs, rather than being read and written at every decoding step. Implemented in vLLM for Mamba-2 and Gated DeltaNet, it accelerates standard decoding by 1.20–1.47x over a tuned vLLM baseline at batch size 256 and delivers about 1.8x the throughput of vLLM's speculative decoding on two 120B-class hybrid models. State I/O is a major bottleneck for hybrid SSM-attention models during inference, and ReplaySSM's input-caching approach roughly halves the dominant state memory traffic while making speculative rollback a cheap buffer-pointer operation. This could significantly improve serving efficiency and speculative decoding for large hybrid models, benefiting systems and ML researchers working on efficient inference. The separation of output computation from checkpoint updates allows a low-precision checkpoint to serve output computation on most steps while a higher-precision checkpoint handles recurrent updates, so rounding error in the low-precision copy never enters later updates. This also enables output-only verification without materializing intermediate states, and separate state precisions reduce output KL divergence and downstream accuracy losses compared with a shared low-precision checkpoint.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: State-space models (SSMs) such as Mamba maintain a fixed-size recurrent state that is updated at each decoding step, which can create a memory bottleneck in hybrid models that combine SSMs with attention. Speculative decoding accelerates LLM inference by using a small draft model to propose tokens that are then verified by a larger target model, but it typically requires cheap rollback of the target model's state when draft tokens are rejected. Quantizing recurrent states to low precision can cause accuracy degradation because quantization errors propagate through successive state updates.

## [Discrete Graph Diffusion Models Secretly Memorize Training Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13786) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.13786) shows for the first time that discrete graph diffusion models (GDMs) exhibit "conditional memorization": when the reverse diffusion trajectory is conditioned on a corrupted training graph, the model reconstructs it almost perfectly, while a held-out test graph is not recovered. This bias is invisible under standard unconditional sampling and sample-level metrics such as edge overlap and WL kernel similarity, and it coincides with a growing generalization gap that early stopping cannot fix. The finding challenges the widely held assumption that graph diffusion models generalize rather than memorize, and it implies that standard sample-level evaluation can miss privacy leakage in graph generative models. It is directly relevant to researchers working on graph generation, privacy, and generalization, and could push the community to adopt trajectory-level or conditional diagnostics. The analysis focuses on two-block stochastic block models (SBMs) and shows that the onset of memorization always precedes the emergence of community structure in generated graphs, so every earlier checkpoint produces structureless graphs. The behavior holds across all tested node sizes, and the memorization surfaces only when the reverse trajectory is conditioned on a corrupted training graph, not during ordinary inference.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Discrete graph diffusion models, such as DiGress, generate graphs by progressively adding or removing edges and changing node/edge categories with a Markovian noise process, then training a graph transformer to reverse this corruption. The stochastic block model is a standard random-graph benchmark that produces community structure by assigning nodes to blocks with different edge probabilities. The Weisfeiler-Lehman (WL) kernel is a graph similarity measure based on iterative neighborhood aggregation, often used to compare generated and training graphs.

## [FORTE: A Production Framework for Feature Deprecation in Recommender Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13806) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers present FORTE, a model-agnostic production framework for discovering, validating, and removing features in industrial recommender systems. It uses efficient perturbation for daily feature evaluation and grouped ablation to handle feature interactions, validated on two large-scale systems where it removed 43.3% of features in a search-relevance model and cut trainable parameters by 29.9% in a ranking model. Feature deprecation is a persistent pain point in production ML, where removing features must not regress any metric while competing for A/B test space. FORTE's offline results are predictive enough of online A/B tests to let deprecations proceed with confidence, potentially saving significant training and serving costs for large-scale recommender systems. FORTE evaluates candidate feature sets built from all low-rank features via grouped ablation, and lineage tracing identifies all downstream consumer models to realize full savings. On a search-relevance model it produced nested candidate sets of 32, 48, and 59 features; online guardrails rejected the largest, and the 48-feature set was selected. On a ranking model, removing 8 candidates improved serving throughput by 19.8-25.5% and reduced annual training costs by hundreds of thousands of dollars.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Recommender systems rely on hundreds or thousands of engineered features, and over time some become redundant or harmful. Ablation is a model-agnostic technique that measures feature importance by removing or perturbing features and observing the impact on metrics, while grouped ablation extends this to sets of interacting features. Low-rank features refer to features with low intrinsic dimensionality or those derived from low-rank approximations, which are common in large-scale models.

## [MC-EvoBench Audits Experience-Driven Self-Improvement in Minecraft Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13829) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers released MC-EvoBench, a controlled Minecraft benchmark with 19 registered evolution chains (12 primary evaluation chains and 7 calibration/probe chains) plus three graph-aligned paired probe families for transfer, designed to test whether embodied agents convert interaction history into reusable capabilities. It evaluates no-prior, related, unrelated, and shuffled source conditions under matched environment-step budgets and five seeds, reporting TSR, WGP, and VMP metrics with retained provenance records. This benchmark addresses a core challenge in embodied recursive self-improvement: distinguishing genuine capability gains from carried-over resources, unequal source exposure, and early execution failures. It provides the AI/ML community, especially embodied AI and agent evaluation researchers, with a controlled testbed for measuring durable, transferable capabilities. The benchmark represents each task as a task-state graph, defines stage-level environment verifiers, assigns matched source and target roles, resets the target world, and permits only a declared agent artifact to cross the source-target boundary. Results reveal a consistent direct-capability gap between controller and planner-backed variants, while related experience yields no stable transfer—it does not improve either tool probe, its small wood-species gain hinges on one seed, and all intervals include zero; stage audits show early wood-collection and crafting bottlenecks often leave later capabilities unobserved.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Recursive self-improvement refers to a hypothesized process where AI systems enhance their own capabilities, potentially leading to rapid intelligence growth; embodied recursive self-improvement applies this idea to agents acting in physical or simulated worlds. Minecraft has become a popular testbed for embodied agents because it offers open-ended tasks like gathering wood, crafting tools, and building, requiring long-horizon planning and skill acquisition. Existing Minecraft benchmarks such as PillagerBench and MCU evaluate agent performance, but MC-EvoBench specifically isolates whether experience transfers as reusable capability rather than merely carried-over resources.

## [SpatialASM Internalizes Active Spatial Mentalization in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13832) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2610.13832) introduces SpatialASM, a scalable framework that internalizes Active Spatial Mentalization (ASM) into Large Vision-Language Models through training-time spatial simulation. It uses an allocentric novel-view generator to ground hypothetical viewpoints in visual evidence and provides two complementary forms of supervision: explicit ASM trajectories and automatically-constructed perspective-taking tasks. Spatial reasoning remains a major weakness of current LVLMs, limiting their use in embodied AI, robotics, and autonomous driving. SpatialASM shows performance gains across seven spatial benchmarks on four LVLM backbones while preserving general vision capabilities, and its ASM trajectory synthesis offers a scalable way to alleviate the scarcity of spatial Chain-of-Thought supervision. The framework employs an allocentric novel-view generator to ground hypothetical viewpoints in visual evidence, and the two supervision forms teach LVLMs when and how to adaptively invoke view planning and imagination during spatial reasoning. Experiments span seven benchmarks from perception-oriented spatial understanding to multi-image, cross-view, and viewpoint-transformation reasoning, with average accuracy improving across all seven.

**Background**: Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) combine visual perception with language understanding but still struggle with 3D spatial reasoning tasks such as judging distances, sizes, and object relationships across viewpoints. Active Spatial Mentalization (ASM) refers to the human-like ability to mentally revisit a scene from task-relevant viewpoints and reconstruct its spatial layout. SpatialASM aims to internalize this ability into LVLMs during training rather than relying on external tools or inference-time tricks.

## [Task Context Reshapes Attention Head Roles in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13895) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.13895) shows that identical attention heads in large language models take on different functional roles depending on the task context, using graph-fact interventions across five open-weight models spanning four families and 1.5–14 billion parameters. In Gemma-2-9B and Qwen3-14B, a single activation edit switches which graph fact the model reads while the written instruction stays fixed, and the same heads then respond to different facts and influence later components differently. This finding challenges the assumption that identifying shared attention heads is enough to explain their function, suggesting that a network-level account of task-dependent influence is needed to understand how models reuse parameters for different computations. It could also guide targeted interventions that alter one behavior while preserving others, which is directly relevant to AI safety and model steering. The authors construct two linear response maps per prompt—one before and one after an activation edit—where each map predicts a later head's response from a measured change in an earlier head. For a separate factual intervention, the condition-specific map reduces mean squared prediction error by 53.6% in Gemma and 5.0% in Qwen compared with averaging the two maps, though the abstract is truncated and the Qwen improvement is notably smaller.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Mechanistic interpretability aims to reverse-engineer neural networks by examining weights, activations, and attention patterns rather than treating models as black boxes. Attention heads are components that move information between token positions, and prior work has identified shared heads reused across tasks. Activation interventions—editing internal activations at inference time—are an established technique for steering model behavior, and this paper combines these ideas to study how task context changes what shared components do.

## [Hide and Seek: Concept Attribution for Black-Box Vision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13935) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (arXiv:2610.13935) proposes Hide and Seek (HnS), a concept-level attribution method that interprets black-box vision models without access to model weights or labeled concept datasets. HnS uses a vision-language model to retrieve concept-aligned images, localizes the concept in each image, masks that region, and measures attribution from the change in the black-box model's output. Existing concept-based attribution methods are limited to classification and regression models and typically require model weights or large concept-annotated datasets, which makes them impractical for proprietary or deployed systems. By removing these requirements, HnS could make concept-level interpretability applicable to a much wider range of black-box vision models and tasks. HnS was validated on CelebA and COCO-Stuff, where it showed better agreement with ground-truth attributions than existing methods, and it was demonstrated on image captioning and satellite image segmentation, tasks where no existing concept attribution method can be used. The paper is an arXiv preprint without peer review, and anonymous code was provided in the supplementary material.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Concept-based attribution explains a model's prediction by assigning importance scores to human-understandable concepts (such as 'smiling' or 'outdoor') rather than raw pixels. Black-box vision models are models whose internal weights and architecture are inaccessible, so interpretation must rely only on observing input-output behavior. Vision-language models (VLMs) jointly process images and text, which lets them connect visual regions with textual concepts and enables HnS to retrieve and localize concepts without labeled data.

## [Critical Review Maps Micro-to-Macro Validity Gap in LLM Social Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.19364) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2507.19364v4) presents a critical review of LLMs used as cognitive or decision-making components in social simulation, introducing the 'Micro-to-Macro Validity Gap' framework to describe how micro-level limitations such as hallucinations, stochastic inconsistency, representational biases, and alignment effects propagate, amplify, or contract into agent-level distortions and macro-level risks like artificial consensus and behavioral homogenization. It also identifies evaluative vulnerabilities including the 'Fluency Fallacy' and automation bias, and examines theory-driven hybrid architectures that embed LLMs within explicit Agent-Based Modeling (ABM) frameworks. As LLM-driven multi-agent simulations grow from narrative sandboxes to population-scale platforms, this framework offers computational social scientists and AI safety researchers a structured way to reason about systemic risks that emerge when scaling inherently flawed models. It helps distinguish contexts where LLM agents add operational value, such as serious games and exploratory modeling, from confirmatory research and precise social forecasting where unconstrained models may be epistemically unsafe. The paper argues that even hybrid architectures embedding LLMs in mechanistic, reproducible ABM frameworks do not guarantee epistemic control, since validity requires multi-level evaluation of environmental dynamics, individual behavior, cross-level interactions, and aggregate outcomes, and such systems remain vulnerable to 'physics washing'. It specifically highlights persona convergence as an agent-level distortion and artificial consensus as a macro-level risk.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Social simulation uses computational models, often agent-based models (ABMs), to study how individual behaviors aggregate into societal patterns. LLMs are increasingly used as the 'brains' of simulated agents because they can generate human-like text and decisions, but they are known to hallucinate, behave stochastically, and reflect training-data biases. Computational social science is the interdisciplinary field that uses such computational methods to study social phenomena, and AI safety concerns the risks of deploying advanced AI systems.

## [DeltaLLM: Delta-Key Sparse Attention Accelerates Edge LLM Prefilling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.19608) ⭐️ 7.0/10

DeltaLLM introduces a training-free, algorithm-hardware-dataflow co-designed framework that accelerates prefilling attention for on-device LLM inference. It achieves up to 60% Delta-Key sparsity and 63% structured attention-score sparsity with negligible accuracy loss, delivering a 1.62x speedup over TeLLMe-v2 on the KV260 FPGA board while reducing DSP and BRAM usage. Attention is the dominant bottleneck during prefilling for edge LLM inference, and existing pruning methods target GPUs rather than resource-constrained edge devices. DeltaLLM's training-free approach and hardware co-design could make long-prompt on-device AI more practical without fine-tuning or pipeline changes. The method uses a causal-attention-aware K-only delta formulation to enable Delta-Key reuse across query heads under Grouped-Query Attention (GQA), and a context-aware hybrid strategy that preserves exact attention near the diagonal while applying delta approximation to nonlocal regions. A vectorized structured sparse online softmax (VSSOS) converts fine-grained spatial score sparsity into hardware-friendly column-level skipping, supported by a specialized vector-processor architecture and unified sparse/dense attention dataflow.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: On-device LLM inference is increasingly important for edge platforms, but high computational and memory demands make it challenging, especially with long prompts. LLM inference has two phases: prefill, which processes the input prompt in parallel and is compute-bound, and decode, which generates tokens one by one and is memory-bound. Grouped-Query Attention (GQA) reduces memory and data movement by letting multiple query heads share a smaller set of key and value heads, and is widely used in modern LLMs.

## [QXR Framework Repairs Global Inconsistencies in Natural-Language Claim Sets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.13600) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper formalizes the problem of global consistency repair for natural-language claim sets and introduces QXR, an adaptive diagnosis-and-repair framework that uses an LLM as a noisy set-level consistency judge to localize minimal conflicting subsets and delete a small hitting set of claims. Evaluated across LLM judges and benchmarks derived from VitaminC, FEVER, and conflicting-evidence RAG, QXR preserves substantially more valid claims than direct all-at-once judging while maintaining high precision. This work addresses a gap in factuality methods by moving beyond pairwise verification to set-level coherence, which is critical for RAG and NLP systems that aggregate claims from multiple sources. It suggests that factuality in claim aggregation should be treated as a set-level repair problem, potentially improving the reliability of generated reasoning and retrieved evidence. QXR treats the LLM as a noisy judge, localizes minimal conflicting subsets, and repairs by deleting a small hitting set of claims; it is evaluated on benchmarks derived from VitaminC, FEVER, and conflicting-evidence RAG, showing higher preservation of valid claims than all-at-once judging while maintaining high precision. The abstract is truncated, and no community discussion is provided, limiting assessment of impact and engagement.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Natural language processing systems often aggregate claims from retrieved passages, documents, and generated reasoning steps, but current factuality methods mostly verify claims individually or compare pairs of claims. This leaves open the question of whether the entire aggregated claim set can be retained coherently, as claims may be locally plausible and pairwise compatible while still being jointly inconsistent. The problem of global consistency repair aims to preserve the largest coherent subset while localizing the compact sources of inconsistency. QXR uses a minimal hitting set approach, which is a concept from set cover problems where a hitting set contains at least one element from each conflicting subset.
