## [AI RTL Verification Plans Fail Semantic Validation: Only 9 of 1,857 Pass](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19844) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces SecTB-RTL, an auditable framework covering 31 tasks and 124 authored hardware-security regressions, and reports a validation incident in which a frozen confirmatory run (C1-R3) completed 1,860 calls; the provider accepted 1,857 responses, but only nine passed the production semantic validator. Because the generation and execution rules did not match, the authors preserve the run as an instrument-validation incident and report no prompt-effect estimate. This incident shows that provider or schema acceptance does not establish execution validity, a critical reliability gap for anyone using LLMs to generate hardware verification artifacts. It affects hardware security teams, EDA toolchains, and AI reliability researchers who may otherwise misreport infrastructure behavior as model behavior. A deterministic non-AI baseline killed 36, 75, and 78 mutants at increasing resource limits, and the first confirmatory run (C1-R2) failed before model execution because the provider rejected its response schema; after a schema-only repair made without viewing outcomes, C1-R3 still failed semantic validation. The authors note that compilation and coverage are only diagnostics, and a subsequent follow-up was excluded because it did not satisfy the preregistered evidence-completeness gate.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: RTL (register-transfer level) verification plans, often called Vplans, define what gets verified, how, and the criteria for completion before testbenches are written, acting as a contract between design and verification teams. Hardware security regression testing re-runs security-critical checks to ensure a system that previously met a requirement still does, and semantic validation checks whether a response is correct in meaning rather than merely matching a schema or exact wording.

## [RLVR²: Verifiable Rubric-Based Ranking for Multi-Criterion RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23457) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.23457v2) proposes RLVR², a verifiable ranking paradigm that replaces the common practice of normalizing each rubric criterion and taking a linear combination with criterion-specific within-group ordinal outcomes, from which a latent utility is recovered and merged into a single training signal. Across three model scales and 16 benchmarks, RLVR² consistently outperforms representative rubric-based baselines, achieving the best overall performance on most benchmarks at every scale. Rubric-based RLVR is increasingly used to train LLMs on multifaceted quality requirements beyond math and code, but the standard linear scalarization of rubric scores silently assumes cardinal comparability and compensability across semantically heterogeneous criteria. RLVR² offers a more principled aggregation that could improve reward modeling and alignment pipelines for researchers and practitioners working on rubric-based reinforcement learning. RLVR² retains only within-group ordering and discards raw score magnitudes, avoiding calibration of heterogeneous rubric scales, and it supports objective-preserving attribute adjustment so auxiliary attributes correlated with observed rankings can enter estimation without expanding the rubric or being directly rewarded. The abstract is truncated and no community discussion is available to gauge impact.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) trains models using objective, programmatically checkable signals such as unit tests or formal proofs, and has proven effective for math and coding. As RLVR expands to less structured domains, multi-dimensional rubrics are used to score responses, but policy optimization consumes only one scalar per rollout, so multiple criterion scores must be aggregated. The prevailing approach normalizes each criterion and takes a linear combination, which implicitly assumes cardinal score differences are comparable across criteria and that gains on one criterion can compensate for failures on another.

## [EpiMind: Graph World Model for Constrained Epidemic Policy Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35545) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces EpiMind, a graph world model framework that combines a graph-factored recurrent state-space model (GF-RSSM) with graph-temporal ADMM to plan epidemic interventions across regions. It reduces admission RMSE by 29% versus graph-free dynamics modeling and plans within 1-5% of the best feasible constant policy while guaranteeing shared-budget feasibility. Epidemic policy planning across regions must account for mobility-driven spillovers and limited shared resources, and existing methods either lack action-conditioned models of coupled dynamics or cannot guarantee per-period feasibility. EpiMind's combination of learned graph-structured dynamics with explicit constrained coordination offers a practical path toward more effective and deployable interventions for public-health decision makers. The GF-RSSM shares neural parameters across regions while each region maintains its own recurrent belief, generating joint policy-conditioned rollouts; graph-temporal ADMM then optimizes regional interventions, enforces shared-resource feasibility through projection, and evaluates temporal specifications under the learned model. The approach outperforms all deployable baselines across three resource budgets in real-context evaluation, though results are demonstrated in simulation rather than real-world deployment.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: World models are learned internal simulators that let reinforcement-learning agents predict future states and plan in imagination rather than by trial and error in the real environment. Recurrent state-space models (RSSMs) compress an agent's history into a latent belief state, and graph neural networks extend this by sharing parameters across nodes (here, regions) while letting each node keep its own recurrent state. ADMM (alternating direction method of multipliers) is a classical optimization algorithm that splits a large constrained problem into smaller subproblems, and in EpiMind it is adapted to a graph-temporal setting to coordinate regional interventions under a shared budget.

## [MS-GLA Adds Multi-Scale Temporal Heads to Gated Linear Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35664) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.35664v2) introduces Multi-Scale Gated Linear Attention (MS-GLA), which distributes attention heads across multiple temporal resolutions and adds a learnable, input-dependent fusion layer to recombine head-group outputs at each timestep. The authors report up to 18.9% improvement on recall-intensive tasks and 9.5% lower average perplexity on language modeling benchmarks compared with GLA at matched parameter counts. The work targets a fundamental representational bottleneck in efficient linear-attention models, which are increasingly positioned as alternatives to standard Transformers for long-context and low-cost inference. If validated, multi-resolution head decomposition could become a general design pattern for linear recurrent architectures such as GLA, RetNet, and Mamba-style models. MS-GLA pools longer token spans in coarser head groups to specialize toward long-range dependencies while finer head groups retain local syntactic sensitivity, expanding effective memory capacity without increasing per-head state size. The approach adapts principles from Multi-Scale State-Space Models (MS-SSM) to the gated linear attention setting, and the paper is a preprint that has not yet undergone peer review.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Gated Linear Attention (GLA) Transformers replace standard softmax attention with a linear recurrent mechanism that uses data-dependent gating, giving linear-time inference and competitive language modeling performance versus LLaMA-style Transformers, RetNet, and Mamba. In GLA, each attention head maintains a fixed-capacity memory matrix that processes tokens one at a time at a single temporal resolution, so the same state must encode both local syntax and long-range semantics. Multi-scale modeling, long used in state-space models and temporal convolutional networks, processes sequences at several resolutions to capture patterns at different time scales.

## [FACT builds articulated digital twins via agentic fidelity loops](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37067) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers released FACT (Fidelity-Aware Construction of Articulated Twins), an agentic framework that iteratively builds articulated digital twins by running an evidence-diagnosis-revision loop over a shared editable representation. It reconstructs editable articulated geometry from images, repairs collision proxies via task-aware local repartitioning before fidelity-constrained compression, and fits response models from passive-response videos using simulation residuals. Visually plausible articulated assets often fail during contact interactions or produce inaccurate motion, so a framework that explicitly targets geometry, contact, and dynamic fidelity could make digital twins more reliable for robotics simulation, manipulation training, and virtual commissioning. It also points toward agentic pipelines that automate asset construction rather than relying on manual modeling and tuning. FACT selects measurements and model edits using quantitative feedback, while numerical tools execute and validate updates; experiments report improved geometric reconstruction over baselines, more reliable interaction with simpler collision proxies, and better reproduction of held-out physical responses than direct parameter inference. The abstract does not specify datasets, metrics, or compute requirements, and the fidelity-constrained compression step trades proxy complexity against simulation accuracy.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Articulated digital twins are editable virtual replicas of objects with joints, such as robot arms, drawers, or doors, used in robotics and simulation. Building them typically requires reconstructing geometry from images, defining simplified collision proxies for physics engines, and estimating dynamic parameters so motion matches reality. Prior work such as ArticulatedGS learns part-level shape and motion self-supervised, while collision proxies and physics-constrained compression are established techniques for keeping simulation fast and stable.

OpenCollab is a new multi-agent coding framework that unifies organization design, enforces experimental control on a shared runtime, and tracks execution via fine-grained event streams. It introduces an 'Adherence' metric showing that changing any single configuration dimension shifts adherence from 47.2% to as high as 97.2%, and its Duo configuration achieves new SOTA results against Mini-SWE-agent, OpenHands, Codex CLI, and Claude Code. Existing multi-agent coding evaluations assume the configured organization is followed faithfully, which prevents clear attribution of performance gains; OpenCollab's Adherence metric and controlled runtime make causal evaluation possible. This matters for AI/ML and software engineering researchers who need to understand whether collaboration structure or underlying components drive results. The framework tracks execution through fine-grained event streams and enforces experimental control on a shared runtime, enabling programmable collaboration. Notably, OpenCollab's single-agent configuration uses the fewest tokens across all evaluated suites, suggesting efficiency gains even without multi-agent collaboration.

<a id="item-259"></a>
## [Coding Agents Can Auto-Evolve Robot Agent Harnesses, Study Finds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39304) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.39304v2) shows that an optimizer coding agent can automatically improve the harness — prompts, tools, and control rules — of a visual-interface robot agent that observes a browser-based 3D interface via screenshots and acts by posing a virtual target gripper. The authors report two key findings: held-out success rises as the training batch grows from 5 to 100 rollouts per round, and a Champion-Challenger rule that only promotes a revision if it beats the incumbent on fixed cases raises held-out success on LIBERO tasks from 51% to 67% over 30 rounds. This work suggests that the scaffolding around an LLM-based robot agent — not just the underlying model — can be optimized automatically, offering a practical path to better robot policies without hand-engineering. The reported 71.2% on the RoboCasa365 leaderboard benchmark, 13.1 points above the best published entry, indicates these gains can translate into state-of-the-art performance and could influence how future agent harnesses are built and evaluated. The evolved harness was combined with a pretrained policy through a single tool that hands over control, reaching 71.2% on RoboCasa365. The paper emphasizes that the optimizer agent must not be given free rein: accepting every proposed revision degrades the harness within a few rounds, and the gains depend on both the rollout scale behind each decision and the selection rule for revisions.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: An agent harness is the surrounding scaffolding — prompts, tools, and control rules — that determines how an LLM agent perceives and acts in an environment; for visual-interface robot agents, the agent sees a browser-based 3D interface through screenshots and acts by posing a virtual target gripper. Until now, such harnesses have been written by hand. Harness evolution is an iterative search procedure in which candidate harnesses are repeatedly evaluated and revised using task feedback, and the Champion-Challenger pattern is a standard model-management technique that promotes a new version only if it outperforms the incumbent.

<a id="item-260"></a>
## [Sample Value Depends on the Learner, Not Just the Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00221) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.00221v2) shows experimentally that the crossover budget m0 between easy-sample and coverage-sample selection is set by the learner, not the data. On low-resolution ImageNet-100, doubling ResNet-18 width moves the crossover from 57 to 85 samples per class on identical frozen subsets, while enlarging the input grid from 32 to 64 pixels moves it back from 85 to 57. This challenges a common assumption in data selection and active learning that sample difficulty or usefulness is an intrinsic property of the data. It implies that selection strategies must be evaluated against the specific learner that will consume the data, affecting how practitioners design data-efficient training pipelines. The grid-induced shift can be reproduced or removed by a single stride change and shrinks as width grows; under the native ImageNet-1k protocol a 4x width range moves the boundary by at most a few samples per class. On a Vision Transformer, coverage samples win at every measured budget, even on easy subsets that work well for the convolutional learner.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: In data selection, one often chooses between 'easy' samples that a short training run learns first and 'coverage' samples that spread across the class in feature space. Prior work often treats sample value as a property of the data, but this paper tests whether that value instead emerges from the interaction between sample and learner. ImageNet-100 is a 100-class subset of ImageNet commonly used for cheaper experiments, and ResNet-18 is a standard convolutional network whose width and input resolution can be varied.

<a id="item-261"></a>
## [StepRS-GRPO: Stepwise Risk-Sensitive RL for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00661) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces StepRS-GRPO, a variant of Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) that varies the risk coefficient of the group-advantage transformation across denoising states in diffusion large language models (dLLMs). Across multiple dLLM backbones and mathematical reasoning benchmarks, it improves both pass@1 accuracy and pass@k coverage over centered GRPO while increasing answer diversity. This work bridges two active research threads — diffusion language models and reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) — by showing that terminal feedback can be reused more effectively across the parallel denoising decisions of dLLMs. If the gains generalize, it could make RL post-training for non-autoregressive language models more sample-efficient and produce more diverse reasoning traces. For binary rewards, the authors show the transformation is exactly a prompt- and state-dependent rescaling of centered outcome advantages, with a capability-based calibration suggesting the coefficient scale and endpoint/interpolation ablations guiding schedule selection. Mass-matched controls and RMS-matched advantage controls support the contributions of state allocation and schedule direction, and reasoning-trace diagnostics show diversity gains extend beyond final-answer strings.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Diffusion language models (dLLMs) generate text by iteratively denoising a sequence or successive blocks, allowing several tokens to be revealed in parallel rather than one at a time like autoregressive models. GRPO is a reinforcement learning algorithm that scores a group of sampled answers per prompt and rewards each answer for beating the group average, and it gained prominence through DeepSeek-R1 and DeepSeekMath. RLVR uses automatically verifiable rewards, such as checking whether a math answer is correct, instead of learned reward models or human annotation.

<a id="item-262"></a>
## [SSR Turns Multimodal Agent Reasoning into Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01892) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.01892v2) introduces Selection-based Structured Reasoning (SSR), a framework that reformulates an agent's reasoning as selection among pre-specified, reusable natural-language candidates rather than free-form generation. Evaluated on seven multimodal search benchmarks with 2B and 4B models, SSR cuts per-turn reasoning latency by over 90% and total per-question inference latency by 28-54% while matching same-scale leading search agents on success rate. Small multimodal models often waste capacity on verbose reasoning that barely guides action, so replacing generation with candidate selection could make search agents far cheaper to run at scale. If the efficiency gains hold across reinforcement learning and supervised fine-tuning objectives, this approach may become a practical recipe for deploying compact multimodal agents in latency-sensitive applications. SSR requires no auxiliary task head: the model scores candidates by their likelihood given the current context, and because the reasoning traces are pre-specified, teacher-forced prefilling can compute token likelihoods in parallel within and across candidates using a shared context KV cache. The results are reported as an arXiv preprint with a project page, and the abstract does not specify the exact candidate set size or how candidates are authored.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Multimodal search agents are models that iteratively gather textual and visual evidence, deciding at each turn what action to take, such as issuing a search query or opening a page. Conventional agents first generate a free-form chain of reasoning and then act on it, which is expensive because every reasoning token must be decoded autoregressively. The KV cache is the stored key-value attention state from the prefill phase that lets a transformer reuse context instead of recomputing it, and SSR exploits this by scoring many fixed candidates against one shared context.

## [GOTT: A Reusable Cross-Embodiment Primitive for Dexterous Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03861) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose GOTT, a reach-acquire-move framework built around a single cross-embodiment contact-acquisition primitive that takes a robot-agnostic object trajectory and a reach specification, brings the hand near a task-relevant contact region, establishes stable contact via a shared closed-loop primitive, and then tracks the desired object motion with a pose-conditioned controller. Simulation and real-world experiments show robust contact across diverse objects, arm-hand platforms, and seen and unseen hand morphologies, consistently improving end-to-end task success over open-loop grasp execution. Dexterous hands still lack a reusable low-level primitive that reliably establishes contact across tasks and embodiments, which is a key bottleneck for turning manipulation intent from foundation models and large-scale human data into real multi-fingered robot behavior. By decoupling local contact control from arm-specific reaching and downstream tracking, GOTT offers a step toward more generalizable, embodiment-agnostic manipulation policies. The framework decomposes dexterous execution into three stages — reach, acquire, and move — where the shared closed-loop primitive establishes stable contact from an approximate initialization, and reach specifications can come from future-aware planning, external models, or human demonstrations while the primitive and tracking backend remain unchanged. The approach is validated in both simulation and real-world experiments across diverse objects and arm-hand platforms, including unseen hand morphologies.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Cross-embodiment learning aims to transfer learned behaviors across robots with different joint counts, action dimensions, and sensor suites, but achieving such generalization remains a fundamental challenge. In robotic manipulation, the central problem is physical contact with the world, and low-level primitives — reusable building blocks for actions like grasping or insertion — have long been studied as a way to make contact-rich tasks more reliable. GOTT combines these ideas by providing a single contact-acquisition primitive that works across different hand and arm morphologies.

## [Green-Routed Neural Operator Uses PDE Physics to Guide Feature Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05337) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced the Green-Routed Neural Operator (GRNO), which uses the governing PDE to determine where latent features are sampled by constructing a departure map whose values serve as read coordinates. Across five two- and three-dimensional PDE systems, GRNO achieved the lowest mean final relative L2 error on four under 40-step autoregressive evaluation and remained competitive on the Keller-Segel model. This work addresses a fundamental mismatch between how PDEs use transport fields to select read coordinates and how neural operators typically treat them only as input values, offering a structural prior that improves long-horizon forecasting. The approach could enhance accuracy in scientific machine learning applications such as climate modeling, fluid dynamics, and other PDE-governed simulations. GRNO employs a parameter-free equation adapter to evaluate the diagnostic relation and a multiscale encoder-decoder that combines centered and routed reads of latent features. Ablations show that supplying the transport field only as an input feature, substituting a learned displacement, or applying the route with spatial misalignment all yield higher errors across all five systems.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Neural operators are a class of deep learning models that learn mappings between function spaces, enabling them to solve families of partial differential equations (PDEs) without retraining for each instance. In many PDEs, transport fields (such as velocity in advection equations) determine which spatial coordinates are relevant for reading information, but standard neural operators treat these fields merely as additional input channels. The Green-Routed Neural Operator draws inspiration from Green's functions, which describe the impulse response of linear differential operators, to align the network's sampling with the physical role of transport fields.

## [TRIAGE Stabilizes Native NVFP4 Reinforcement Learning for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07043) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.07043) introduces TRIAGE, a direction-aware stabilization method that mitigates mismatch-induced instability in native NVFP4 reinforcement learning. It characterizes how learner-sampler mismatch interacts with the policy-gradient direction, distinguishing locally amplifying from contracting updates, and selectively rebalances updates at the segment level while retaining native NVFP4 W4A4 execution on both sampler and learner. Low-precision execution can substantially accelerate RL for large language models, but mismatch between learner and sampler execution can destabilize policy optimization, so a targeted stabilization method could make efficient 4-bit RL training practical. Experiments on Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-30B-A3B show stable optimization and full-precision-level performance across five mathematical reasoning benchmarks, with up to 2.3x higher rollout throughput than BF16. In native NVFP4 runs, the authors observe an early imbalance between two amplifying regions favoring negative-advantage, negative-gap updates, with tail tokens concentrated in a small fraction of response segments before mismatch spreads globally. TRIAGE modifies the optimization objective and applies bounded repair to residual severe mismatch, while retaining native NVFP4 weight-and-activation 4-bit (W4A4) forward execution on both sampler and learner.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: NVFP4 is a 4-bit floating-point data format (1 sign bit, 2 exponent bits, 1 mantissa bit, E2M1) introduced in NVIDIA's Transformer Engine, designed to accelerate training and inference while maintaining stability. Reinforcement learning for large language models typically uses a sampler to generate rollouts and a learner to update the policy; when these run in different precisions, the resulting mismatch can bias or destabilize policy-gradient updates. TRIAGE targets this specific failure mode in native NVFP4 RL.

## [ELF-BO Uses Evaluation Idle Time for Fully Bayesian Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08969) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose ELF-BO, an algorithm that samples from the hyperparameter posterior while the objective function is being evaluated, then reweights those samples once the objective value is observed. Across synthetic functions and real-world applications, ELF-BO matches fully Bayesian methods while keeping decision latency on par with or better than standard Bayesian optimization. Fully Bayesian optimization gives better uncertainty estimates in the low-data regime but has been prohibitively expensive, so it is rarely used in practice. By hiding that computation inside the unavoidable objective evaluation latency, ELF-BO makes fully Bayesian optimization practical for real-world black-box problems such as hyperparameter tuning and expensive simulations. The key trick is that hyperparameter posterior samples are drawn during the objective evaluation and only need reweighting once the observed objective value arrives, so no substantial decision-time cost is added. The paper reports results on both synthetic functions and real-world applications, though it is an arXiv preprint without peer review or released code mentioned in the abstract.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Bayesian optimization is a sequential model-based strategy for globally optimizing black-box objective functions whose evaluations are costly, such as running an experiment or a numerical simulation. It typically uses a Gaussian process surrogate, and standard practice fits the surrogate's hyperparameters with a point estimate by maximizing the marginal likelihood. A fully Bayesian alternative averages over hyperparameter uncertainty, which improves uncertainty estimates but usually requires expensive Markov chain Monte Carlo sampling, making it too slow for routine use.

## [AO-DA Decouples Logic from Persona in Edge LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09772) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.09772v2) introduces the Decoupling Architecture (AO-DA), which splits logical inference ('What') from persona expression ('How') into two inference paths running on a single INT4 base model with hot-swappable LoRA adapters. In 480 ablation runs on an Apple M2 laptop with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct and Gemma-3-4B-it, the decoupled logic path stayed structurally invariant to context pollution, producing byte-identical outputs across all pollution levels (40/40), while the mixed single-pass baseline degraded monotonically. This offers a practical architectural fix for persona-logic interference, a known failure mode in edge LLM agents where long, persona-heavy histories corrupt structured reasoning. Because it runs on consumer hardware with INT4 quantization and enables persona hot-swapping in 1.7 ms without re-running the logic path, it could be immediately useful for deploying robust, personalized agents at the edge. The logic path receives only the core turn and emits a verifiable structured state (Micro-State), keeping its prompt fixed at 180 tokens (Llama) or 167 tokens (Gemma) while the mixed prompt grows from 242 to 1,203 tokens on Llama. Separation costs one extra decode on a topic's first turn (28.2 s vs 18.2 s on Llama), and the robustness advantage holds on the 8B model (failure 0-20% vs 25-95% at L2-L4) but not on the 4B model, where both approaches collapse.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Edge LLM agents must simultaneously maintain a persona and reason correctly within a single context window that accumulates history and persona instructions, a setup prone to 'context pollution' where irrelevant or misleading content degrades reasoning. LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation) adds small trainable weights to a frozen base model, enabling cheap task or persona switching, while INT4 quantization compresses model weights to 4-bit integers to cut memory and enable deployment on consumer devices like laptops.

## [PHRBench: Benchmarking How LLMs Recover from Hallucinated Premises](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10455) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced PHRBench, a controlled benchmark that behaviorally evaluates post-hallucination reasoning across four domains and 18 large language models, spanning 4,820 controlled instances. The benchmark labels each reasoning trajectory as Hallucination Compliance, Hallucination Avoidance, or Heuristic Correction, and finds that successful recovery is relatively rare and correlates with more frequent belief updates. Hallucinated information can propagate through multi-stage LLM pipelines and contaminate downstream reasoning, so understanding when models can recover is critical for building reliable AI systems. PHRBench offers a methodological contribution that could shape future work on LLM reliability, self-correction, and multi-step reasoning evaluation. The benchmark separates behavioral labels from final-answer correctness, defining an insightful trajectory as one that successfully corrects and reaches the right answer. A lightweight predictor using properties of the hallucinated prompt achieved an AUROC of 0.847 for predicting successful recovery.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Large language models sometimes generate hallucinated content—plausible but false information—that can be fed back into later reasoning steps in multi-stage systems. Post-hallucination reasoning (PHR) studies how models handle such erroneous context, but prior work mainly looked at final outcomes rather than response-level behavior. Belief revision research similarly examines whether models update their beliefs when presented with new or contradictory evidence.

## [Higher-Order Action Supervision Boosts Policy Robustness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11175) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.11175v2) proposes a loss scheme that supervises both zeroth-order actions (action labels) and first-order actions (action dynamics) simultaneously, claiming to dramatically improve policy performance and control robustness in imitation learning and reinforcement learning. The method is a lightweight plug-and-play module with formal theoretical guarantees that works with any off-the-shelf policy model (deterministic, stochastic, or flow policies) without structural modifications. Control instability and poor robustness are major obstacles to deploying learning-based policies in real-world robotics and autonomous driving, so a general method that improves temporal consistency and out-of-distribution generalization could meaningfully ease practical deployment. Because it integrates with existing offline RL frameworks and policy classes without architectural changes, it has the potential for broad adoption across the RL and robotics communities. The approach supervises higher-order action dynamics to enforce temporal consistency, and the authors report substantial performance and robustness gains on OGBench and D4RL continuous control benchmarks, including improved out-of-distribution generalization in low-data regimes. The paper claims formal theoretical guarantees and compatibility with deterministic, stochastic, and flow-based policies, though it remains a preprint without community discussion or independent replication yet.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Imitation learning trains a policy to mimic expert demonstrations, while reinforcement learning trains a policy through trial-and-error interaction with an environment; both typically optimize only the action label at each step, known as zeroth-order action supervision. First-order action refers to the rate of change or dynamics of actions over time, so supervising it encourages smoother, more temporally consistent behavior. OGBench and D4RL are widely used benchmark suites for offline reinforcement learning and continuous control, and out-of-distribution generalization measures how well a policy performs on situations not seen during training.

## [ComCLIP: Fixing Contrastive Post-training for CLIP via Temperature Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11374) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.11374v2) revisits the claim that standard contrastive loss causes catastrophic forgetting in CLIP post-training, finding that the real culprit is an inappropriate contrastive temperature τ rather than insufficient negatives. The authors propose ComCLIP, a single-epoch recipe that freezes the text encoder and trains the vision encoder with a properly-tempered InfoNCE loss, an MSE anchoring loss, and a relational distillation loss from DINOv2. This work challenges the recent trend of abandoning contrastive objectives in favor of distillation for CLIP post-training, showing that a lightweight, properly-tuned contrastive recipe can match or exceed self-distillation baselines. Since CLIP is the de facto vision encoder for downstream VLMs like LLaVA, a drop-in refined encoder with unchanged architecture and inference cost could simplify and improve vision-language model fine-tuning. ComCLIP matches CLIP-Refine on zero-shot classification while significantly improving linear probing transferability (48.99 vs. 42.28 on ViT-B/16) and MMVP on ViT-L/14 (24.20 vs. 19.01), though CLIP-Refine remains stronger on image-text retrieval. Used as a drop-in vision encoder for LLaVA-1.5-7B without re-aligning the projector or LLM, ComCLIP yields no net change across 8 VLM benchmarks, confirming downstream compatibility.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: CLIP (Contrastive Language–Image Pre-training) is a foundational vision-language model trained with a contrastive objective (InfoNCE) that aligns image and text embeddings, and it serves as the default vision encoder for downstream VLMs such as LLaVA. Post-training aims to refine CLIP cheaply, but recent work argued that standard contrastive loss causes catastrophic forgetting under small batches, motivating distillation-based alternatives. The contrastive temperature τ scales the logits in the InfoNCE softmax and controls how sharply the model separates positives from negatives, directly affecting gradient behavior.

## [Onboard YOLOX-S Vessel Detection on Versal Hardware Cuts EO Downlink Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12182) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper trains a YOLOX-S detector on a controlled dataset of 68 annotated Maxar scenes covering 43 vessel classes, then quantizes and deploys it on the DPU of a Versal VC1902 adaptive SoC for onboard maritime object detection. The authors propose several downlink modes and report that on dense harbor and coastal scenes, transmitting tiles containing detections retains 98% of vessels using only 29% of the scene volume, while image crops retain 83% of vessels at just 3% of the volume. Very-high-resolution Earth-observation satellites routinely acquire more data than they can store or downlink, and vessels occupy only a tiny fraction of each maritime scene, so content-aware onboard selection can dramatically reduce downlink pressure. This work shows a practical, end-to-end path—dataset, training, quantization, embedded deployment, and downlink strategy—that could inform future satellite system design for maritime surveillance. The detector suffers only a limited loss of detection quality after quantization and processes each scene in a few seconds on the Versal VC1902 DPU. The proposed downlink modes range from metadata only (box, class, and score per detection) to image crops around vessels, tiles holding detections, and the whole scene with a degraded background, with trade-offs estimated from measured detection errors.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Earth-observation satellites capture imagery at very high resolution, generating far more data than onboard storage and downlink bandwidth can handle, which makes onboard data reduction important. YOLOX-S is a lightweight version of the YOLOX anchor-free object detection family, and AMD Versal adaptive SoCs combine FPGA fabric with AI Engines and a DPU (deep processing unit) IP block for embedded inference. Maxar provides high-resolution commercial satellite imagery, which the authors used to build their annotated vessel dataset.

It also includes chord fingering displays, per-stroke visualization, slow practice down to 20% speed, free chord tools, and tuners, all without signup or download. This project shows how far browser-based audio tools have come, making realistic instrument practice accessible to anyone with a web browser and no installation. It could appeal to beginners and hobbyists who want a low-friction way to practice chords and strumming, while also serving as a showcase for the Web Audio API in music education. ## [UK Per-Capita Electricity Generation Falls to 1970s Levels](https://ourworldindata.org/data-insights/electricity-generation-per-person-in-the-united-kingdom-has-fallen-by-a-third-from-its-early-2000s-peak) ⭐️ 6.0/10

Our World in Data reports that the United Kingdom's per-capita electricity generation has fallen by roughly one-third from its early-2000s peak, dropping to levels last seen in the 1970s. The decline is attributed to improved energy efficiency, deindustrialization, and increased electricity imports through interconnectors. This trend challenges assumptions about ever-rising electricity demand and has significant implications for grid planning, energy policy, and the debate over electrification of heating and transport. It also raises questions about whether the UK is genuinely becoming more efficient or simply offshoring its energy-intensive activities. The reduction is roughly split 50/50 between industrial and residential demand, with efficiency gains from LED lighting and appliances playing a notable role. The UK was a net importer of 33.4 TWh of electricity in 2024, up 40% from 2023, meaning some of the consumption decline is offset by imports rather than reflecting purely lower demand.

**Background**: Per-capita electricity generation measures total electricity produced in a country divided by its population, and is often used as a rough proxy for economic activity and living standards. The UK's electricity system has undergone major changes since the early 2000s, including the closure of coal plants, growth in renewables, and the expansion of interconnectors that allow electricity trade with France, Norway, and other neighbors. Deindustrialization refers to the shift from manufacturing to services, which reduces industrial electricity consumption.

## [EliteTable: LLM-Guided Program Search for Control Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12536) ⭐️ 6.0/10

EliteTable is a self-improving program-search algorithm guided by large language models that maintains a fixed-capacity table of high-scoring solutions to propose new programs, edit existing ones, and remix several solutions. It was evaluated on six Gymnasium control tasks with ten generations and fifty candidate slots per generation, showing median score improvements in all six tasks but mixed held-out transfer results, including declines in six of ten Acrobot-v1 searches. This work sits at the intersection of LLM-guided code generation, evolutionary program search, and reinforcement learning, suggesting that LLMs can iteratively improve executable control policies without gradient-based training. If the approach can be made more reliable, it could offer a complementary route to policy learning for control tasks where reward shaping or gradient methods are difficult. The evaluation used six Gymnasium control tasks, each with ten generations and fifty candidate slots per generation, and each task was run across ten independently initialized searches. In CartPole-v1 the selected winner hit the search-score ceiling in the first generation of all ten searches, while BipedalWalker-v3 and CarRacing-v3 improved without reaching their reporting targets, and the paper notes that comparisons and ablations are still needed to establish relative performance and component contributions.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Program search refers to algorithms that explore a space of programs, often using evolutionary or synthesis techniques, to find solutions that satisfy a specification or maximize a score. Gymnasium is a maintained fork of OpenAI's Gym library that provides standardized reinforcement learning environments, including classic control tasks such as CartPole-v1, Acrobot-v1, BipedalWalker-v3, and CarRacing-v3. Large language models have recently been used to propose and edit code, enabling search over executable policies rather than neural network weights.

## [LLM Pipeline Builds Faithful Hierarchical Hardware Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12762) ⭐️ 6.0/10

Researchers present an automated LLM-driven pipeline that converts lengthy, heterogeneous hardware datasheets into hierarchical hardware knowledge graphs using chunk-based property extraction, hierarchical routing, and iterative refinement with source provenance preserved. Evaluated on 20 datasheets, it achieves 98.8% property faithfulness versus 76.1% for a one-shot baseline, and fully covers 83.6% of faithful baseline properties while the baseline covers only 10.5% of theirs. Hardware datasheets are essential for component selection, system integration, and compatibility verification, but manual inspection is tedious and error-prone. A reliable automated pipeline could reduce overlooked constraints and incorrect design decisions, benefiting hardware engineers and downstream retrieval and reasoning systems. The staged design reduces per-query context and maintains traceable intermediate representations, with graph updates applied through validated instructions and deterministic local execution. The authors also introduce inter-graph completeness to measure relative semantic coverage without exhaustive ground truth.

**Background**: Hardware datasheets pack dense engineering specifications into tables, figures, and text, making them lengthy and heterogeneous. Large language models can automate extraction, but naive one-shot processing often omits relevant specifications even when the document fits within the model's context window. Hierarchical knowledge graphs organize entities and relations into multi-level structures, capturing both abstract concepts and fine-grained details.

## [DeFiRank: Federated Pairwise Ranking for DeFi Governance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12916) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (arXiv:2610.12916v1) proposes DeFiRank, a federated learning-based pairwise ranking framework that lets multiple institutions collaboratively train a provider-ranking model without sharing private transaction data. Evaluated on a real-world blockchain transaction dataset, it achieves NDCG@15 of about 0.87 versus a centralized baseline of about 0.92, while matching ranking stability (Kendall τ ≈ 0.70) and producing 648 provider promotions and 39 demotions. DeFi governance today often relies on manual committee review, which is subjective, hard to scale, and blind to complex behavioral patterns; a privacy-preserving ranking model could make promotion and demotion decisions more objective and data-driven. If it works, this approach could help DeFi networks reward genuine contributors and filter out suspicious actors without forcing institutions to hand over sensitive transaction data. The framework uses federated learning so that institutions only share model updates rather than raw transaction data, and it ranks providers by signals such as counterparty diversity, use of multiple applications, and transaction frequency. The main caveat is that the federated model's ranking quality is modestly below the centralized baseline, and the paper is an arXiv preprint without peer review or reported evaluation of adversarial or privacy-attack scenarios.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Federated learning is a machine learning technique in which multiple entities collaboratively train a model while keeping their data decentralized, sharing model updates instead of raw data, which is motivated by privacy and data-access concerns. Pairwise ranking is a ranking approach that compares items head-to-head and aggregates the results into an overall ordering. DeFi governance refers to decision-making in decentralized finance protocols, typically by holders of governance tokens, and is increasingly concerned with identifying authentic versus suspicious network participants.

## [Collaborative Learning and Reasoning over Model Slices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12927) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.12927) proposes collaborative learning that replaces a single large model with smaller heterogeneous models under a comparable parameter budget, using model slicing and pyramid supervision to mitigate error propagation in LLM reasoning. Evaluated on multi-hop QA, math reasoning, code generation, and open-ended constrained generation, the trained heterogeneous models match or exceed the original large model and multi-agent baselines while being more inference-efficient. Error propagation is a core weakness of single-model chain-of-thought reasoning, where an early mistake cascades through later steps. If heterogeneous model slices can independently reassess prior reasoning, this could offer a more efficient alternative to multi-agent systems and RL-based collaboration methods, affecting how practitioners design reasoning pipelines under fixed compute budgets. The approach combines protocol distillation with step-level supervision in pyramid supervision to refine both reasoning and interaction decisions, and the paper reports differentiated collaboration patterns across task types. The abstract does not specify model sizes, the exact number of slices, or quantitative efficiency gains, so those details would require reading the full paper.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Large language models often solve complex tasks by generating intermediate reasoning steps, but because each step conditions on previous outputs, an early error can corrupt the entire chain. Multi-agent systems address this by having several models collaborate, yet they typically incur high inference cost. Model slicing here means decomposing one large model into several smaller heterogeneous models within a similar parameter budget, so that prior reasoning can be re-examined from different parameterizations.

## [SWEAR: Difficulty-Aware Audio Reasoning Cuts Tokens](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13081) ⭐️ 6.0/10

SWEAR introduces a difficulty-aware reasoning framework for large audio-language models (LALMs) that derives five-dimensional difficulty scores and compressed reasoning traces from offline assessments, then learns difficulty signals during supervised fine-tuning and uses reinforcement learning to adapt reasoning allocation across stages. Experiments on MMAU-test-mini and MMAR show improved accuracy while substantially reducing generated tokens, shortening reasoning for easy instances without sacrificing performance on difficult ones. This work addresses a key efficiency bottleneck in LALMs, where models often generate excessive reasoning tokens even for simple audio queries, wasting compute and increasing latency. By making reasoning effort adaptive to instance difficulty, SWEAR could make audio-language reasoning more practical for real-time or resource-constrained applications, and its approach may generalize to other multimodal reasoning systems. SWEAR relies on five-dimensional difficulty scores and compressed reasoning traces obtained from complementary offline assessments, rather than coarse-grained difficulty estimation. It combines supervised fine-tuning to learn difficulty signals with reinforcement learning to allocate reasoning across stages, and is evaluated on the MMAU-test-mini and MMAR benchmarks.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Large audio-language models (LALMs) extend large language models with audio encoders to jointly understand speech, environmental sounds, and music, but they often struggle with complex reasoning tasks. Adaptive reasoning methods aim to balance accuracy and efficiency by allocating more or fewer reasoning steps depending on query difficulty, yet many existing approaches use only coarse difficulty estimates. Benchmarks like MMAU and MMAR evaluate expert-level audio reasoning across speech, sound, and music domains.

## [Faster Decisions Don't Guarantee Better LLM Agents, Study Finds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13092) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.13092) empirically evaluates integrating Jev, a fast 'System One' decision model from TypeSafe AI, into five LLM-based agents across 4,500 task executions in ALFWorld and AndroidWorld. Results show Jev substantially cuts decision cost and latency in all five agents, but task success stays near baseline on ALFWorld and declines notably in two mobile agents. The study challenges the assumption that cheaper, faster local decisions automatically translate into better agent outcomes, showing that auxiliary reasoning and extra interactions can absorb the savings. It offers concrete guidance for agent designers to prioritize progress preservation, failure recovery, and completion verification rather than raw decision speed. Across 4,500 executions, Jev reduced decision cost and latency in all five agents, but mean cost per successful outcome actually increased in one agent even as spending per attempt fell. The authors attribute failures to failed recovery and premature completion, and recommend evaluating efficiency by resources per delivered outcome rather than per attempt.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Jev is a 'System One' decision model built by TypeSafe AI that makes fast, structured, low-cost decisions rather than generating conversational text, positioning it as a complement to LLMs that handle open-ended reasoning. ALFWorld is a text-based embodied benchmark aligned with the ALFRED dataset, while AndroidWorld is Google DeepMind's benchmark of 116 programmatic tasks across 20 real Android apps. LLM-based agents rely on repeated decisions to complete tasks, so optimizing each decision's cost and latency seems attractive—but this paper tests whether those local savings actually improve end-to-end task delivery.

## [VETR Framework Enables Self-Improving Agents for Symbolic Graphics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13098) ⭐️ 6.0/10

Researchers introduced VETR (Verified Execution Trajectories for Refinement), a framework that enables recursive self-improvement of visual agents by extracting actionable evidence from execution traces to refine symbolic graphics programs. Evaluated on BlenderGym (280 tasks) and BlenderBench (27 tasks) with GPT-5.6-luna and Qwen3.8-27B, VETR consistently outperformed existing refinement strategies including BlenderAlchemy and VIGA. This work addresses a key bottleneck in agentic visual reasoning: the ambiguity of supervision from final renders alone. By grounding refinement in execution traces, VETR offers a more reliable path toward self-improving agents that can iteratively debug and improve their own symbolic graphics programs, with potential impact on program synthesis and visual reasoning research. VETR retains aligned program/render records with bounded runtime observations and introduces an execution refiner that compiles observed writes and state changes into constrained alternative programs, validating endpoints and preservation conditions against captured runtime state. Notably, repair construction requires no additional language-model calls, though it may require additional executions; controlled ablations evaluate the contributions of execution refinement and role-asymmetric evidence organization under fixed generator-interaction caps.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Symbolic graphics reasoning involves agents that manipulate executable world representations, such as 3D scenes or graphics programs, to achieve visual goals. Execution traces record the step-by-step state changes that occur when a program runs, and prior work in program synthesis has shown that traces provide richer supervision than input/output examples alone. Self-improving agents are AI systems that iteratively refine their behavior through interaction with an environment, and VETR applies this paradigm to symbolic graphics by using execution traces as the basis for refinement.

## [RegulaTutor: Theory-Informed Framework for Long-Term Personalized Tutoring with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13399) ⭐️ 6.0/10

Researchers introduced RegulaTutor, a theory-informed framework that links historical evidence, learner interpretation, and instructional support to enable long-term personalized tutoring with large language models. The paper also presents PairRoute, an offline refinement procedure that uses intermediate execution records to distinguish harness, model, and mixed failures and to propose targeted harness revisions. Existing LLM tutoring systems rely on memory and retrieval but often lack an explicit pedagogical rationale linking learner history to teaching strategies, so RegulaTutor's evidence-linked policies could make personalized tutoring more transparent and adaptive. It also offers a systematic way to diagnose and refine tutoring pipelines, which matters for AI in education as systems move toward lifelong learning support. The tutoring harness operationalizes a cognitive, affective, metacognitive, and motivational (CAMM) framework to derive evidence-supported learner interpretations and teaching policies, and these policies retain links to supporting evidence and update as new observations arrive. PairRoute focuses refinement on historical evidence acquisition, and results show average improvements in historical-evidence accuracy, learner diagnosis, and judged teaching quality across evaluated model configurations.

The CAMM framework extends this by considering cognitive, affective, metacognitive, and motivational processes that learners navigate during study. RegulaTutor builds on these ideas to make LLM-based tutoring policies explicit and evidence-linked rather than relying only on opaque memory or retrieval.

## [Local Agentic AI Framework Generates Edge Cases for Safety Assurance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13479) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2610.13479v1) introduces a laptop-based agentic AI framework that uses local language models to adaptively generate edge case scenarios for autonomous system safety assurance. The framework employs think-act-observe-update-continue loops with lightweight JSON encoding, and a second independent LM critiques each scenario against domain-specific criteria before simulation and metric-based clustering. This work addresses a key limitation of existing LM-based scenario generation, which often relies on human-in-the-loop or rule-based strategies that restrict diversity in the long tail of the distribution. By running locally, the approach offers a cheap, fully controlled, and privacy-preserving alternative that could make safety assurance more accessible and traceable for autonomous systems. The framework uses system requirements, system description, and environment description as knowledge bases in contextual prompts to ensure scenario realism, then exploits metric scores to create similar edge cases or explore novel scenarios. Post-hoc clustering of metric scores and thematic analysis of rich-text scenario descriptions pinpoint functional gaps and their triggering conditions, creating traceable evidence for the safety assurance case.

## [Road MOPD Uses Cross-Teacher Guarding to Fix Gradient Conflicts in Multi-Teacher Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13521) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2610.13521) proposes Cross-Teacher Guarding for Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation (Road MOPD), which introduces non-routed teachers as auxiliary cross-domain critics on the same student trajectories. The method combines cross-teacher-guided token-level credit assignment with preventive multi-teacher gradient projection, and in experiments it mitigates degradation in the disadvantaged domain, gains over 5 percentage points under high conflict, and improves overall performance in low-conflict and three-domain settings. Multi-teacher on-policy distillation is becoming a mainstream way to merge specialized LLM capabilities into a single student model, but joint optimization on shared parameters often causes gradient conflicts and capability degradation. Road MOPD offers a concrete mechanism to keep target-domain learning signals while suppressing conflicting updates, which could make capability integration more stable for practitioners building general-purpose models from domain specialists. The approach has two components: cross-teacher-guided credit assignment, which uses teacher agreement to strengthen jointly supported token-level corrections and attenuate conflicting signals, and preventive multi-teacher gradient projection, which uses auxiliary gradients from the same trajectories to constrain potentially conflicting updates with adaptively controlled projection strength. Reported gains include over 5 percentage points under high conflict, but the results come from a preprint with no community discussion or engagement metrics provided.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student model on responses it generates itself, using a teacher's token-level output distribution as dense supervision instead of sparse rewards. Multi-teacher OPD (MOPD) extends this by routing domain-specific data to specialized teachers so that different capabilities can be integrated into one student. However, because all domains still update shared parameters, teachers with very different response styles and expertise can produce conflicting gradients, a well-known problem in multi-task learning that can degrade performance in some domains.

## [Constraint acquisition tool learns size-independent models from examples](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13640) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.13640) describes a Constraint Acquisition Tool that learns size-independent constraint programs from input data and positive/negative example solutions of multiple problem instances. The system first finds size-specific models using typical patterns over global and binary constraints, then generalizes them into a size-independent model that can run on unseen input data, outputting both MiniZinc code and a textual model description, with feasibility demonstrated on the PTHG21 Challenge problems. This work addresses a key limitation of existing constraint acquisition methods, which typically produce models tied to a specific problem size, by automatically generalizing to size-independent models that work on unseen data. It could reduce the manual modeling burden for constraint programming practitioners and make automated model learning more practical for real-world combinatorial problems. The tool combines pattern-based learning over global and binary constraints with a generalization step, and it produces human-readable textual descriptions alongside executable MiniZinc code. Evaluation is limited to the example problems of the PTHG21 Challenge, so broader scalability and applicability to other problem classes remain to be demonstrated.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Constraint acquisition (also called constraint learning) is a subfield of AI that infers constraint models from examples of feasible and infeasible assignments, aiming to automate the modeling step of constraint programming. MiniZinc is a free, open-source, solver-independent constraint modeling language used to describe combinatorial problems for solvers based on constraint programming, integer programming, SAT, and SMT. The PTHG21 Challenge, presented at the CP 2021 'Progress Towards the Holy Grail' workshop, provides a series of constraint acquisition problems and a standard data/result format for benchmarking such tools.

## [Kairos: A Byte-Level Foundation Model for All Modalities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12495) ⭐️ 6.0/10

Kairos introduces a byte-level foundation model architecture that combines bidirectional gated-DeltaNet linear attention, sliding-window attention over shared projections (LiZAttention2), block-wise Attention-Residual aggregation, a sparse Mixture-of-Experts feed-forward layer, and a multi-scale byte-level codec, trained with a uniform-noise denoising objective over a single byte space shared by text, image, audio, video, lidar, and control streams. The report runs convergence sanity checks, overfitting a single 440-byte sentence at 1-15M parameters with three seeds, and finds that LiZAttention2 and window attention alone reach a minimum loss near 10^-3 at 3,000 steps, with code released on GitHub and PyPI. This work is an early-stage architectural exploration that asks whether a byte-level, multi-modal foundation model can even optimize before asking whether it is useful, and its negative result on shared attention projections (a convergence-speed effect, not a capacity effect) offers a useful caution for other researchers combining linear attention, sliding-window attention, and MoE. If the planned pretraining run succeeds, it could point toward a single unified byte space for text, image, audio, video, lidar, and control streams, but the current report makes no claims about generalization or practical utility. The convergence checks are limited to text-only overfitting of a single 440-byte sentence at 1-15M parameters, and the apparent advantage of shared over separate attention projections disappears with more steps or more diverse data and reverses when projections are frozen. The Discussion analytically sizes a 200M-total/25M-active target but explicitly states that none of its throughput figures are measured, and the authors note that the checks say nothing about generalization, multimodal training, or comparison with existing models.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Linear attention replaces the unbounded key-value cache of softmax attention with a fixed-size recurrent state, reducing sequence mixing to linear time and decoding to constant memory; gated DeltaNet is a recent variant that uses a delta rule to edit this compressed memory. Sliding-window attention restricts each token to attend only to a local window of nearby tokens, which is a common way to extend context length cheaply. Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) layers route each token to only a few specialized feed-forward sub-networks, increasing total parameters while keeping per-token compute low. A byte-level model operates directly on raw bytes rather than a tokenizer, which in principle allows one shared vocabulary across text, images, audio, and other modalities.

## [Spiking Neural Networks Cut Beam Prediction Energy by 10x](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12507) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2610.12507) develops spiking neural network (SNN) architectures for sensing-aided beam prediction in mmWave systems and systematically benchmarks them against conventional artificial neural networks (ANNs) across position-only, vision-only, and multimodal inputs. The multimodal SNN reaches 58.08% top-1 accuracy, beating the closest-energy ANN by 18.11 percentage points, while requiring up to an order of magnitude less energy at comparable accuracy. Beam training overhead is a major bottleneck in mmWave and emerging 6G networks, and sensing-aided prediction can reduce it, but the sensing and inference pipeline itself is energy-hungry. Showing that SNNs can deliver comparable or better accuracy at far lower energy suggests a practical path toward greener edge-deployed beam management, though the accuracy gap for position-only sensing means SNNs are not a universal replacement for ANNs. For position-only sensing, compact ANNs consistently outperform SNNs, so the energy advantage only materializes for vision and multimodal inputs. The authors further show that SNN accuracy is limited by the binary (spike-based) communication between layers rather than by model size, pointing to inter-layer information encoding as the key bottleneck to address.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Spiking neural networks are biologically inspired models that transmit information as discrete spikes over time rather than as continuous activations at every propagation cycle, firing only when a neuron's membrane potential crosses a threshold; this event-driven behavior can be far more energy-efficient on neuromorphic hardware. In mmWave systems, narrow beams require sequentially probing many directions during beam training, which creates large overhead and latency, so sensing-aided beam prediction uses environmental data such as camera images or position to infer the best beam directly. This paper asks whether SNNs can serve as the energy-efficient inference engine for that sensing-aided pipeline.

## [TimesFM 3.0 Tops Benchmark for Day-Ahead Electricity Forecasting in University Building](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12638) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper benchmarks statistical, machine-learning, recurrent neural-network, and pretrained TimesFM models for day-ahead electricity forecasting in a university building, evaluating all configurations on the same 57 test days. TimesFM 3.0 with covariates achieved the lowest overall errors, reducing mean absolute error by approximately 43% relative to weekly seasonal-naive forecasting. This study provides practical evidence that pretrained time-series foundation models like TimesFM can serve as strong zero-shot baselines for building energy forecasting, potentially reducing the need for building-specific model training. It also highlights the trade-offs between accuracy, covariate preparation, and computational cost that practitioners must weigh when selecting forecasting models. XGBoost remained competitive, though paired daily comparisons did not establish a clear MAE advantage for TimesFM; adding covariates improved holiday forecasts but slightly worsened workday and weekend forecasts, leaving overall MAE almost unchanged. TimesFM requires no building-specific weight training, while XGBoost offers rapid local fitting and inference, and the evaluated LSTM took longer to fit without outperforming either approach.

Seasonal-naive forecasting, a simple baseline, sets each forecast equal to the last observed value from the same season.

## [iRAG: Incremental RAG for Human-in-the-Loop Decision Support](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12666) ⭐️ 6.0/10

Researchers introduced iRAG, a human-in-the-loop decision-support system that combines Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) with Hybrid Decision-Making, addressing settings where the knowledge base starts empty and is progressively populated through human decisions. The system stores previous decisions as retrievable precedents and progresses from silent observation to evidence-grounded contestation and finally to autonomous handling with abstention, matching baseline final-decision error under 10% drift and reducing it under 40% drift. Most RAG systems assume a static, pre-built knowledge base, so iRAG's approach of growing the KB from human decisions could enable decision support in domains where no curated corpus exists, such as clinical triage or fraud review. Its explicit stability-plasticity trade-off also highlights a fundamental limitation that any incremental RAG deployment must confront. Retrieval decay improves performance on changed knowledge but increases error on stable knowledge, evidencing a stability-plasticity trade-off rather than a general advantage. Human oversight is an architectural provision of the workflow, but its usability with real human users remains to be evaluated.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) is a technique that lets large language models retrieve relevant text from external sources before generating a response, reducing hallucinations and avoiding costly retraining. Concept drift refers to the phenomenon where the statistical relationship between inputs and outputs changes over time, degrading model performance. The stability-plasticity dilemma describes the tension in learning systems between preserving old knowledge (stability) and integrating new knowledge (plasticity).

Researchers introduced GeoFitBench-LLM, a benchmark that assesses whether large language models can infer the datasets, supporting resources, and constraints required by geo-analytic requests expressed in natural language. Experiments on seven models under minimal prompting showed GPT-5.4 Thinking performed best, recovering the complete set of resources in 66.67% of benchmark requests, while accurate fitness-for-use specification remained harder as inferential demands increased. The benchmark is structured around two reasoning dimensions, Spatio-Temporal Relational and Semantic Grounding, and encodes ground truth requirements in a fixed, provider-independent schema for reproducible evaluation of resource identification and attribute-level specification. Results indicate that identifying necessary resources is often achievable, but accurate fitness-for-use specification becomes more difficult as inferential demands increase.

## [KITE: Knowledge-Injected Temporal Encoder for Rare Event Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12738) ⭐️ 6.0/10

KITE introduces a knowledge-injected temporal encoder that injects semantic priors into a self-supervised event forecaster through two switchable mechanisms: a covariate mechanism and an attention-bias mechanism, each of which is the identity at its null so a single architecture serves every prior type without per-dataset architecture search. Experiments show that an informative prior beats no prior in-domain (a periodicity clock yields +14% pooled h-AUROC, p<0.001; a conservation residual yields +47% at a 0.25 label fraction) and transfers out-of-domain (+53%), while priors already recoverable from the observed window stay inert. Forecasting rare, high-cost events in multivariate time-series systems is difficult because their precursors are seldom observed, leaving purely data-driven models with little to learn from. KITE offers a switchable, attributable route to contextual conditioning that could benefit domains such as predictive maintenance and healthcare, and serves as a substrate for scaling toward cross-domain, pretrained time-series foundation world models that carry their governing structure explicitly. A prior helps only when it supplies information the observed window does not already contain: in a short context an informative prior beats no prior, and a working prior transfers out-of-domain, substituting for feature transfer. Notably, the attention-bias mechanism shows no measurable gain, and priors that the window already recovers stay inert.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Oct 13, 04:00

**Background**: Multivariate time-series event forecasting aims to predict whether a specific event will occur in the near future based on multiple observed variables over time. Rare, high-cost events (e.g., equipment failures or clinical deteriorations) are especially hard because their precursors are seldom observed, so labels are scarce and purely data-driven models have little to learn from. A semantic prior is knowledge held by domain engineers that precedes such events but is absent from the scarce labels. KITE is built on a joint-embedding predictive architecture (JEPA) for multivariate time series and treats a prior as a switchable input to a single self-supervised backbone rather than a change to its architecture. The h-AUROC metric is a variant of the area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUROC), which measures how well a model ranks positive examples above negative ones; a score of 1.0 indicates perfect separation and 0.5 indicates random guessing.

## [COFFEQA: Counterfactual-Guided Few-Shot Distillation for LLM QA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12837) ⭐️ 6.0/10

Researchers propose COFFEQA, a counterfactual-guided few-shot distillation strategy for open-book question answering that uses minimally modified contexts to provide extra supervision around challenging factual samples. They also introduce Context Following Difficulty (CFD), a metric that validates counterfactuals before distillation, and report that on TriviaQA with 32 few-shot pairs, COFFEQA improves over factual-only distillation by 8.77 average EM points and 9.98 average F1 points in the T5 setting. Few-shot distillation is attractive for deploying smaller models in resource-constrained settings, but its performance depends heavily on which samples are chosen, and uncertain samples near the teacher's decision boundary can degrade results. COFFEQA offers a practical way to select and augment training samples, potentially making efficient LLM distillation more reliable across in-domain and out-of-domain QA benchmarks. The method defines a counterfactual as a minimally modified context that causes the teacher to change its answer, and it provides geometric insights on how factual-counterfactual pairs constrain the deviation between teacher and student decision boundaries. Experiments span five in-domain and three out-of-domain QA benchmarks using T5 and Qwen2.5 model families under the same few-shot budget.

**Background**: Knowledge distillation trains a smaller student model to mimic a larger teacher model, which is useful when compute or memory is limited. Few-shot learning aims to learn from only a handful of examples, and counterfactual examples are inputs modified slightly so that a model's output changes, often used to understand or regularize model behavior. COFFEQA combines these ideas for generative open-book QA, where the model answers questions using a provided context.

## [Diffusion Models Applied to Localized Ship Hull Optimization with CFD Validation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12870) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper presents a reward-directed diffusion framework for localized ship hull optimization, using parametric masking to restrict geometric changes to the bulbous bow and forebody while preserving full-hull continuity. An automated viscous free-surface CFD pipeline, validated against measurements for two benchmark container ships, evaluated a generated variant against GenHull E, a naval architect's refinement, showing comparable resistance at Fn=0.195 but 45-50% higher resistance at Fn=0.250 and 0.260. This work demonstrates both the promise and current limitations of applying diffusion models to engineering design, showing that high-fidelity CFD assessment can reveal tradeoffs hidden by aggregate metrics. It highlights the need for improved geometric representations and reward functions that include viscous pressure and stability terms for physically realizable generative designs. The framework uses surrogate-assisted sequential Monte Carlo sampling to target reduced predicted calm-water resistance, with stability retained through training data and displacement matched during screening. The assessment reveals an inability to represent a faired bulbous bow and a reward lacking viscous pressure and stability terms, and notes that aggregating resistance across the friction-pressure crossover can conceal tradeoffs.

**Background**: Diffusion models are a class of generative models that learn to reverse a noise-adding process to produce new samples, and have recently been applied to engineering design tasks. This paper explores using diffusion models to generate localized hull modifications, then validates them with CFD to assess real-world performance.
